\|q_{1}+q_{2}\|<2
.

Proof.

To this end, we first claim that for
\alpha
,
\beta\in\mathcal{Q}_{M_{0}}
with
\alpha\neq 0
,
\|\beta\|=1
,

{\rm Re}\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{\alpha}}{|\alpha|}\beta=1

if and only if
\alpha=t\beta
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
with
t\neq 0
. We only check the “only if”-part. Since
{\rm Re}(\overline{\alpha}\beta/|\alpha\beta|)\leq 1
on
M_{0}
except for finite points (zeros of
\alpha
and
\beta
), we have

0\leq\iint_{M_{0}}\left(1-{\rm Re}\dfrac{\overline{\alpha(z)}\beta(z)}{|\alpha(z)\beta(z)|}\right)|\beta(z)|dxdy=1-{\rm Re}\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{\alpha}}{|\alpha|}\beta=0

from the assumption. Since
|\beta|>0
and
\overline{\alpha}\beta/|\alpha\beta|=|\alpha|\beta/|\beta|\alpha
almost everywhere on
M_{0}
, the imaginary part of a meromorphic function
\beta/\alpha
vanishes almost everywhere on
M_{0}
. From the identity theorem, we obtain
\alpha=t\beta
for some
t\in\mathbb{R}
with
t\neq 0
.

Let us return to the proof of the proposition.
Let
q_{1}
,
q_{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{M_{0}}
with
\|q_{1}\|=\|q_{2}\|=1
.
When
\|q_{1}+q_{2}\|=2
,
q_{1}+q_{2}\neq 0
and

\displaystyle 2=\|q_{1}+q_{2}\|

\displaystyle={\rm Re}\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{1}+q_{2}}}{|q_{1}+q_{2}|}(q_{1}+q_{2})

\displaystyle={\rm Re}\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{1}+q_{2}}}{|q_{1}+q_{2}|}q_{1}+{\rm Re}\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{1}+q_{2}}}{|q_{1}+q_{2}|}q_{2}\leq\|q_{1}\|+\|q_{2}\|=2.

From the above argument, we deduce that
t_{1}q_{1}=q_{1}+q_{2}=t_{2}q_{2}
for some
t_{1}
,
t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
with
t_{1}\neq 0
and
t_{2}\neq 0
. Since
\|q_{1}\|=\|q_{2}\|=1
and
q_{1}+q_{2}\neq 0
, we obtain
q_{1}=q_{2}
.
∎

3.2.2.
Trivializations of tangent bundle and cotangent bundle

Since the Teichmüller space is biholomorphic to a bounded domain in
B_{2}=B_{2}(\mathbb{D}^{*},\Gamma_{0})
(with the notation in §
3.1.3
), the (holomorphic) tangent bundle
T\mathcal{T}_{g}
is naturally identified with the trivial bundle
\mathcal{T}_{g}\times B_{2}\to\mathcal{T}_{g}
.

A basis
\varphi_{1}
,
\cdots
,
\varphi_{3g-3}\in B_{2}
of
B_{2}

gives a global (holomorphic) trivialization of
\mathcal{Q}_{g}=T^{*}\mathcal{T}_{g}
defined by

\mathcal{Q}_{g}\ni q\mapsto(x,(\langle D(\mathscr{B}_{x_{0}}^{-1})|_{x}[\varphi_{1}],q\rangle,\cdots,\langle D(\mathscr{B}_{x_{0}}^{-1})|_{x}[\varphi_{3g-3}],q\rangle))\in\mathcal{T}_{g}\times\mathbb{C}^{3g-3},

where
x\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
with
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{x}
, and
D(\mathscr{B}_{x_{0}}^{-1})
is the differential of the inverse of the Bers embedding. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
is the dual of
B_{2}
, for any
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}
, there is a unique
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
such that

(3.2.4)

\langle(D(\mathscr{B}_{x_{0}}|_{x})^{-1}[\varphi_{k}],q\rangle=\langle(D(\mathscr{B}_{x_{0}}|_{x_{0}})^{-1}[\varphi_{k}],Q\rangle=\langle[\mathscr{A}_{x_{0}}(\varphi_{k})],Q\rangle

for all
k=1
,
\cdots
,
3g-3
. Threfore, we obtain a natural holomorphic identification

(3.2.5)

T^{*}\mathcal{T}_{g}\cong\mathcal{Q}_{g}\ni q\to(x,Q)\in\mathcal{T}_{g}\times\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}.

We can easily check that the identification (
3.2.5
) is independent of the choice of the frame
\{\varphi_{1}
,
\cdots
,
\varphi_{3g-3}\}
of
B_{2}
.

3.2.3.
Teichmüller Beltrami differentials

The
Teichmüller Beltrami differential
on
M_{0}
is a
(-1,1)
-bounded measurable form of the form

k\dfrac{\overline{q}}{|q|}=k\dfrac{\overline{q(z)}}{|q(z)|}\dfrac{d\overline{z}}{dz}.

for
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{M_{0}}
and
k>0

(e.g.
[
3
, p.29]
).
The Teichmüller Beltrami differential have an extremal property, called the infinitesimal unique extremal property. Namely, when
\mu\in L^{\infty}_{(-1,1)}(M_{0})
satisfies

\llangle\mu,\varphi\rrangle=\llangle\overline{q}/|q|,\varphi\rrangle

for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{Q}_{M_{0}}
, then
\|\mu\|_{\infty}\geq 1
, and the equality holds only if
\mu=\overline{q}/|q|
almost everywhere on
M_{0}
(cf.
[
22
, §4.10, Corollary 2]
and the discussion below). Furthremore, each tangent vector
v\in T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
admits a unique Teichmüller Beltrami differntial as a representative.

The quasiconformal mapping defined from the Teichmüller Beltrami differntial
k\overline{q}/|q|
with
0<k<1
is called the
formal Teichmüller map
(cf.
[
32
, §5.2.2]
).
A formal Teichmüller map defined from a pair
(k,q)
with
k=\|q\|
is called the
Teichmüller map
.
Formal Teichmüller maps have an extremal property.
Namely, any formal Teichmüller map
f
on
M_{0}
is uniquely extremal in the sense that the maximal dilatation of
f
attains minimal only at
f
among all quasiconformal mappings on
M_{0}
homotopic to
f
(e.g.
[
22
, §4.7, Corollary 1]
and
[
32
, Theorem 5.9]
).

3.2.4.
Teichmüller metric

For
x=(M,f)\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
, the
Teichmüller metric

\boldsymbol{\tau}
is a Finsler metric on
\mathcal{T}_{g}
defined by

(3.2.6)

\boldsymbol{\tau}(x,v)=\sup\left\{{\rm Re}\,\langle v,q\rangle\mid\|q\|=1,q\in\mathcal{Q}_{M}\right\}

for
v\in T_{x}\mathcal{T}_{g}
. By definition, the Teichmüller metric on
T\mathcal{T}_{g}
is thought of as the dual (Finsler) metric to the
L^{1}
-norm on
\mathcal{Q}_{g}\cong T^{*}\mathcal{T}_{g}
(cf.
[
68
, §2]
).

Royden shows that the Teichmüller metric coincides with the Kobayashi metric on
\mathcal{T}_{g}
, and that the Teichmüller metric is of class
C^{1}
on the tangent bundle except for the zero section (cf.
[
68
, Lemma 3, Theorem 3]
). We will deal with the regulality of the Teichmüller metric in §
11.2
.

The length distance with respect to the Teichmüller metric on
\mathcal{T}_{g}
is called the
Teichmüller distance
on
\mathcal{T}_{g}
. The Teichmüller space with the Teichmüller distance is a unique geodesic and complete metric space which is deduced from the Teichmüller uniqueness theorem and the compactness of the family of quasiconformal mappings with uniformly bounded maximal dilatation.

3.3.
Kodaira-Spencer theory

3.3.1.
Notation on the sheaf cohomology

We first fix notations. For a sheaf
\mathscr{S}
over a topological space
M
and an open set
U\subset M
, we denote by
\Gamma(U,\mathscr{S})
the set of sections of
\mathscr{S}
over
U
. Let
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a locally finite covering of
M
. For a
k
-chain
\sigma\in C^{k}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{S})
, we denote by
\sigma_{i_{0}\cdots i_{k}}\in\Gamma(I_{i_{0}}\cap\cdots\cap U_{i_{k}},\mathscr{S})
the
(i_{0}\cdots i_{k})
-component of
\sigma
.

Let
\delta\colon C^{k}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{S})\to C^{k+1}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{S})
denote the coboundary operator. Let
Z^{k}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{S})
and
B^{k}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{S})
be the spaces of cocycles and coboundaries, and
H^{k}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{S})
the
k
-th cohomology group with coefficient
\mathscr{S}
. For instance,
H^{0}(M,\mathscr{S})=\Gamma(M,\mathscr{S})
.
It is known that when a covering
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
satisfies
H^{1}(U_{i},\mathscr{S})=0
for
i\in I
, the natural inclusion

H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{S})\to H^{1}(M,\mathscr{S})

defined from the direct limit is isomorphic and

H^{2}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{S})\to H^{2}(M,\mathscr{S})

is injective (cf.
[
43
, Theorems 3.4 and 3.5]
).

Let
M
be a Riemann surface.
Let
\Theta_{M}
and
\Omega_{M}
be the sheaves of germs of holomorphic vector fields and holomorphic
1
-forms on
M
, respectively. For
p,q,r\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathscr{S}=\Theta_{M}
or
\Omega_{M}
, we denote by
\mathcal{A}^{p,q}(\mathscr{S}^{\otimes r})
the
sheaf of germs of
C^{\infty}
-
(p,q)
-forms with coefficients in
\mathscr{S}^{\otimes r}
. For instance, a section of
\mathcal{A}^{p,q}(\Omega_{M}^{\otimes r})
(resp.
\mathcal{A}^{p,q}(\Theta_{M}^{\otimes r})
) on a domain
V\subset M
is a smooth
(p+r,q)
-form on
V
(resp. smooth
(p-r,q)
-forms), respectively. Therefore, there are natural identifications
\mathcal{A}^{p,q}(\Omega_{M}^{\otimes r})=\mathcal{A}^{0,q}(\Omega_{M}^{\otimes(p+r)})
and
\mathcal{A}^{p,q}(\Theta_{M}^{\otimes r})=\mathcal{A}^{0,q}(\Omega_{M}^{\otimes(p-r)})
. For instance,
\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Omega_{M_{0}})
is the sheaf of germs of smooth area forms on
M_{0}
.

Let
\mathcal{U}=\{(U_{i},z_{i})\}_{i\in I}
be a locally finite analytic chart of
M
.
For
\mathscr{S}=\Theta_{M}
or
\Omega_{M}
, the anti-holomorphic derivative
\overline{\partial}
defines a sheaf homomorphism

\begin{CD}C^{k}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{p,0}(\mathscr{S}))@>{\overline{\partial}}>{}>C^{k}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{p,1}(\mathscr{S}))\\
\sigma=\{\sigma_{i_{0}\cdots i_{k}}\}_{i_{0},\cdots,i_{k}\in I}@>{}>{}>\overline{\partial}\sigma=\{(\sigma_{i_{0}\cdots i_{k}})_{\overline{z}}\}_{i_{0},\cdots,i_{k}\in I}\end{CD}

3.3.2.
The first cohomology group as the tangent space

The following exact sequence

(3.3.1)

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>\Theta_{M_{0}}@>{inc}>{}>\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}})@>{-\overline{\partial}}>{}>\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}})@>{}>{}>0,\end{CD}

where “inc” means the inclusion,
leads the Dolbeault theorem

(3.3.2)

H^{1}(M_{0},\Theta_{M_{0}})\cong\Gamma(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))/\overline{\partial}\Gamma(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))

(cf.
[
43
, Theorem 3.13]
).
The canonical isomorphism
H^{1}(M_{0},\Theta_{M_{0}})\cong T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
follows from the coincidence of the right-hand sides of (
3.2.1
) and (
3.3.2
), which is verified from the Serre duality theorem (or the non-degeneracy of the Serre pairing defined by the residue map). See
[
32
, Theorem 7.9]
and
[
19
, §17]
. See also §
13.1
for the discussion on the sign of the
\overline{\partial}
-operator in (
3.3.1
).

For reader’s convenience, we shall give an explicit correspondence.
We fix a covering
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
M_{0}
with
H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\cong H^{1}(M_{0},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
For
X\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,
take
\xi\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{0}))
such that
\delta\xi=X
. Then
\mu=-\overline{\partial}\xi_{i}
on
U_{i}
is naturally thought of as an element in
\Gamma(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\subset L^{\infty}_{(-1,1)}(M_{0})
, and

(3.3.3)

\begin{CD}H^{1}(M_{0},\Theta_{M_{0}})\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\ni[X]@>{\mathscr{T}_{x_{0}}}>{}>[\mu]=[-\overline{\partial}\xi_{i}]\in T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}\end{CD}

is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphism. See the discussion in
[
43
, Theorem 5.4]
.

3.3.3.
Presentation of the pairing by the residue

The exact sequence of sheaves

(3.3.4)

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>\Omega_{M_{0}}@>{}>{}>\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M_{0}})@>{d}>{}>\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Omega_{M_{0}})@>{}>{}>0\end{CD}

leads the isomorphism

(3.3.5)

H^{1}(M_{0},\Omega_{M_{0}})\cong H^{0}(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Omega_{M_{0}}))/dH^{0}(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M_{0}})).

This yields the residue map

{\rm Res}\colon H^{1}(M_{0},\Omega_{M_{0}})\to\mathbb{C}

defined by

(3.3.6)

{\rm Res}([\omega])=\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\iint_{M_{0}}\Omega(z)d\overline{z}\wedge dz

for
[\omega]\in H^{1}(M_{0},\Omega_{M_{0}})
,
where
\Omega\in H^{0}(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Omega_{M_{0}}))
is the corresponding area form via the isomorphism (
3.3.5
).
Namelly, there is
\Omega^{\prime}\in C^{0}(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M_{0}}))
such that
d\Omega^{\prime}=\overline{\partial}\Omega^{\prime}=\Omega
and
\delta\Omega^{\prime}=\omega

(cf.
[
19
, §17.1]
).

Convention 2
.

As (
3.3.6
), in this paper, we adopt
d\overline{z}\wedge dz
as the basis of
2
-forms.
The reason why we use
d\overline{z}\wedge dz
as a basis of
2
-forms comes from the exact sequence (
3.3.4
). Namely, for
\omega=\omega(z)dz
,
d\omega=\omega_{\overline{z}}(z)d\overline{z}\wedge dz
. See
[
19
, (c) in §15.9]
.

The pairing (
3.2.2
) is presented with the residue as

(3.3.7)

\displaystyle\langle v,q\rangle

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2i}\iint_{M_{0}}(-(\xi_{i})_{\overline{z}})qd\overline{z}\wedge dz=-\dfrac{1}{2i}\iint_{M_{0}}d(\xi_{i}qdz)

\displaystyle=-\pi\,{\rm Res}([Xq])

since

\delta(\{\xi_{i}q\}_{i\in I})=Xq=\{X_{ij}q\}_{i,j\in I}\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Omega_{M_{0}})
,
where
v=[X]=[\{X_{ij}\}_{i,j\in I}]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\cong T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
, and
\xi=\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
with
\delta\xi=X
. In particular,
when
H^{1}(U_{i},\Theta_{M_{0}})=0
for
i\in I
, the residue pairing

\begin{CD}H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\times\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}@>{}>{}>\mathbb{C}\\
([X],q)@>{}>{}>-\pi\,{\rm Res}([Xq])\end{CD}

is non-degenerate.

3.4.
Stratification of the space of quadratic differentials

Let
Z
be a manifold. A
stratification
of
Z
is a locally finite collection of locally closed submanifolds
\{Z_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of
Z
, the
strata
, indexed by a set
J
such that

(1)

Z=\cup_{j\in J}Z_{j}
; and

(2)

Z_{j}\cap\overline{Z_{k}}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
Z_{j}\subset\overline{Z_{k}}
.

(cf.
[
15
]
). The conditions induce a partial order on
J
, where
j\leq k
if
Z_{j}\subset\overline{Z_{k}}
.

Each
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}
determines a topological data
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n};\epsilon)
where

k_{1}
,
\cdots
,
k_{n}
are the orders of zeros:
\epsilon=+1
if
q
is the square of an abelian differentiall
\epsilon=-1
otherwise. A stratum
\mathcal{Q}_{g}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n};\epsilon)
consists all quadratic differentials determining the data
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n};\epsilon)
. The principal stratum
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
corresponds to
(1,\cdots,1;-1)
. The principal stratum
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
is open and generic, and each stratum is a complex submanifold of
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
(cf.
[
78
]
. See also
[
15
]
). The index of the stratum satisfies
\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}=4g-4
. In general, some data
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n};\epsilon)
cannot be realized by a holomorphic quadratic differential. See Masur-Smillie
[
54
]
.

Any point in each stratum admits a local complex chart defined by the relative period of the abelian differentials defined from the square roots of the differential. Hence, the
L^{1}
-norm function is real analytic on each stratum because the
L^{1}
-norm is written as the quadratic form of the period.

For the record, we summery as follows.

Proposition 3.4.1
.

The
L^{1}
-norm function on
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
is real-analytic on each stratum

Chapter 4
Teichmüller space of tori

In this chapter, we devote to discuss the model case (the most simplest case) of our results.
We treat the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{1}
of tori.
The case of once punctured tori is treated in the same way as that in the case of flat tori. Indeed, the canonical completion at the puncture gives an isomorphism between the Teichmüller space of once punctured tori and that of flat tori. The case of four puncture sphere is treated in a similar way.

4.1.
Teichmüller space of tori

The Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{1}
of tori is the space of the Teichmüller equivalence classes of marked complex tori of dimension
1
, where a
complex torus
of dimension
1
is a Riemann surface homeomorphic to a torus. For
\tau\in\mathbb{H}=\{\tau\in\mathbb{C}\mid{\rm Im}(\tau)>0\}
, let
\Gamma_{\tau}=\langle 1,\tau\rangle
be a
\mathbb{Z}
lattice in
\mathbb{C}
generated by
1
and
\tau
, and set
M_{\tau}=\mathbb{C}/\Gamma_{\tau}
. Let
\Sigma_{1}=M_{i}
(
i
is the imaginary unit). Let
f_{\tau}\colon\Sigma_{1}\to M_{\tau}
be a homeomorphism with
(f_{\tau})_{*}(1)=1
and
(f_{\tau})_{*}(i)=\tau
. Then,
(M_{\tau},f_{\tau})
is a marked complex torus, and

\mathbb{H}\ni\tau\mapsto(M_{\tau},f_{\tau})\in\mathcal{T}_{1}

is bijective. It is well-known that this identification become a biholomorphic under a canonical complex structure on
\mathcal{T}_{1}
.

4.2.
Tangent and cotangent bundles

For
(M_{\tau},f_{\tau})\in\mathcal{T}_{1}
, the space
\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}
of holomorphic quadratic differentials on
M_{\tau}
is described by

\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}=\{q=q\,dz^{2}\mid q\in\mathbb{C}\}

where the coordinate
z
is induced from
\mathbb{C}
via the projection
\mathbb{C}\to M_{\tau}=\mathbb{C}/\Gamma_{\tau}
since any
\Gamma_{\tau}
-periodic holomorphic function on
\mathbb{C}
is constant. We identify the space
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
of holomorphic quadratic differentials with
\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}
by

(4.2.1)

\mathcal{Q}_{1}\ni((M_{\tau},f_{\tau}),-2i\,q\,dz^{2})\mapsto\left(\tau,q\right)\in\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}.

The constant “
-2i
” in the trivialization (
4.2.1
) is a regulation which makes the pairing between
T\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
to be the ordinary complex Euclidean pairing as we see at
(
4.2.4
).
The
L^{1}
-norm function
\boldsymbol{n}
on
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
is given by

\boldsymbol{n}(\tau,q)=\dfrac{1}{2i}\iint_{M_{\tau}}|-2iq|d\overline{z}\wedge dz=2|q|{\rm Im}(\tau).

The
L^{\infty}_{(-1,1)}(M_{\tau})
consists of bounded measurable
(-1,1)
-form
\mu^{\prime}=\mu^{\prime}(z)d\overline{z}/dz
such that
\mu^{\prime}(z)
is periodic in terms of the action of the lattice
\Gamma_{\tau}
. Then,
\mu^{\prime}
is infinitesimally equivalent to a unique constant
(-1,1)
-form
(\mu/{\rm Im}(\tau))d\overline{z}/dz
with

\displaystyle\mu=\dfrac{1}{2i}\iint_{M_{\tau}}\mu^{\prime}(z)d\overline{z}\wedge dz.

We notice that the Beltrami differential
\mu(t)
of the quasiconformal (affine) deformation from
M_{\tau}
to
M_{\tau+t\mu}
(
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
) behaves

(4.2.2)

\mu(t)=t\dfrac{i\mu}{2{\rm Im}(\tau)}\dfrac{d\overline{z}}{dz}+o(t)

as
t\to 0
. Hence, we define a trivialization of
T\mathcal{T}_{1}
by

(4.2.3)

T\mathcal{T}_{1}\ni\left((M_{\tau},f_{\tau}),\left[\dfrac{i\mu}{2{\rm Im}(\tau)}\dfrac{d\overline{z}}{dz}\right]\right)\mapsto\left(\tau,\mu\right)\in\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}

Under the coordinates of
T\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
, the pairing function is

(4.2.4)

\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}((\tau,\mu),(\tau,q))=\dfrac{1}{2i}\iint_{M_{\tau}}\dfrac{i\mu}{2{\rm Im}(\tau)}(-2iq)d\overline{z}\wedge dz=\mu q.

Therefore, the Teichmüller metric on
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, which is the dual Finsler metric with respect to the
L^{1}
-norm function, is obtained by

\boldsymbol{\tau}(\tau,\mu)=\sup\left\{{\rm Re}\left(\mu q\right)\mid\boldsymbol{n}(\tau,q)=1\right\}=\dfrac{|\mu|}{2{\rm Im}(\tau)},

which implies that the Teichmüller metric on
\mathcal{T}_{1}
is nothing but the Poincaré metric on the upper-half plane
\mathbb{H}
with curvature
-4

(cf.
[
29
, Theorem 6.6.5]
and
[
40
, Chapter IV, Proposition 1.3]
).

The Teichmüller Beltrami map
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}\colon\mathcal{Q}_{1}\to T\mathcal{T}_{1}
is presented by

\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}(\tau,q)=\left((M_{\tau},f_{\tau}),\left[2i\,{\rm Im}(\tau)\overline{q}\dfrac{d\overline{z}}{dz}\right]\right)=(\tau,4\overline{q}{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2}).

In this case, it is immediately checked that the Teichmüller Beltrami map is a real-analytic diffeomorphism from
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
to
T\mathcal{T}_{1}
.

4.3.
Second order infinitesimal spaces and Infinitesimal Duality

All the second order infinitesimal spaces of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
are holomorphically trivial to
\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
. We identify

\displaystyle TT\mathcal{T}_{1}\ni\xi\partial_{\tau}|_{(\tau,\mu)}+\zeta\partial_{\mu}|_{(\tau,\mu)}\mapsto(\tau,\mu,\xi,\zeta)\in\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle T^{*}\!T\mathcal{T}_{1}\ni\kappa d\tau|_{(\tau,\mu)}+\lambda d\mu|_{(\tau,\mu)}\mapsto(\tau,\mu,\kappa,\lambda)\in\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle T\mathcal{Q}_{1}\ni\alpha\partial_{\tau}|_{(\tau,q)}+\beta\partial_{q}|_{(\tau,q)}\mapsto(\tau,q,\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle T^{*}\!\mathcal{Q}_{1}\ni\gamma d\tau|_{(\tau,q)}+\eta dq|_{(\tau,q)}\mapsto(\tau,q,\gamma,\eta)\in\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}.

The flip, the switch, and the dualization are

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}(\tau,\mu,\xi,\zeta)

\displaystyle=(\tau,\xi,\mu,\zeta)

\displaystyle{\bf Swh}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}(\tau,q,\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=(\tau,\alpha,\beta,q)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}(\tau,q,\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=(\tau,q,\beta,-\alpha).

From (
4.2.4
),
Proposition

2.7.1
and
Proposition

2.8.1
, the pairing functions and the holomorphic symplectic form are

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{T\mathcal{T}_{1}}((\tau,\mu,\xi,\zeta),(\tau,\mu,\kappa,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\xi\kappa+\zeta\lambda

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{Q}_{1}}((\tau,q,\alpha,\beta),(\tau,q,\gamma,\eta)

\displaystyle=\alpha\gamma+\beta\eta

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}((\tau,q,\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}),(\tau,q,\alpha_{2},\beta_{2})

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{1}{2}(\alpha_{1}\beta_{2}-\beta_{1}\alpha_{2}).

Thus, the pairings are presented as those in the case of trivial bundles. This is caused from the regulation of the trivializations of
T\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
so that the pairing between
T\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
is presented as the ordinary Euclidean case as (
4.2.4
).

The differentials of the
L^{1}
-norm function
\boldsymbol{n}
on
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
and the Teichmüller metric
\boldsymbol{\tau}
on
T\mathcal{T}_{1}
are obtained by

\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{n}|_{(\tau,q)}

\displaystyle=-i|q|d\tau+{\rm Im}(\tau)\dfrac{\overline{q}}{|q|}dq=\left(\tau,q,-|q|,{\rm Im}(\tau)\dfrac{\overline{q}}{|q|}\right)

\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\tau}|_{(\tau,\mu)}

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{|\mu|}{4i{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2}}d\tau+\dfrac{\overline{\mu}}{4|\mu|{\rm Im}(\tau)}d\mu=\left(\tau,\mu,-\dfrac{|\mu|}{4i{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2}},\dfrac{\overline{\mu}}{4|\mu|{\rm Im}(\tau)}\right).

Hence, we see that the
L^{1}
-norm function and the Teichmüller metric are of class
C^{1}
except for the zero sections. In particular,

\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{n}^{2}|_{(\tau,q)}

\displaystyle=-4i|q|^{2}{\rm Im}(\tau)d\tau+4\overline{q}{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2}dq=\left(\tau,q,-4i|q|^{2}{\rm Im}(\tau),4\overline{q}{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\tau}^{2}|_{(\tau,\mu)}

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{|\mu|^{2}}{4i{\rm Im}(\tau)^{3}}d\tau+\dfrac{\overline{\mu}}{4{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2}}d\mu=\left(\tau,\mu,-\dfrac{|\mu|^{2}}{4i{\rm Im}(\tau)^{3}},\dfrac{\overline{\mu}}{4{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2}}\right).

Therefore, at
(\tau,q)=-2iq\,dz^{2}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\tau}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle{\bf Swh}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}\circ({\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}})^{-1}(-\partial\boldsymbol{n}^{2}|_{(\tau,q)})

\displaystyle={\bf Swh}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}\circ({\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}})^{-1}\left(\tau,q,4i|q|^{2}{\rm Im}(\tau),-4\overline{q}{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle={\bf Swh}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}\left(\tau,q,4\overline{q}{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2},4i|q|^{2}{\rm Im}(\tau)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\tau,4\overline{q}{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2},4i|q|^{2}{\rm Im}(\tau),q\right)

\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\tau}^{2}|_{\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}(\tau,q)}

\displaystyle=\partial\boldsymbol{\tau}^{2}|_{(\tau,4\overline{q}{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2})}

\displaystyle=\left(\tau,4\overline{q}{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2},4i|q|^{2}{\rm Im}(\tau),q\right).

This coincidence implies that the infinitesimal duality holds in this model case. We will discuss in §
11.4
for general cases.

4.4.
Levi convexities and CR structures of the unit sphere bundles

We will discuss in §
11.1.3
the structure of the (real) tangent space of the unit sphere bundle

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{g}=\{q\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}\mid\boldsymbol{n}(q)=1\}

in
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
. Here we deal with the simplest case
g=1
.

The real tangent space
T^{\mathbb{R}}_{q}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}

at
q\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}
is presented as

T^{\mathbb{R}}_{q}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}=\{V\in T_{q}\mathcal{Q}_{1}\mid{\rm Re}(\partial\boldsymbol{n}|_{q}(V))=0\}.

It is easy see that
i\partial_{q}=(0,i)\in T_{q}\mathcal{Q}_{1}
is in the real tangent space
T^{\mathbb{R}}_{q}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}
.
In this case, the horizontal subspace
T_{q}^{H}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}
coincides with the maximal complex subspace of the (real) tangent space
T^{\mathbb{R}}_{q}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}
and

T_{q}^{H}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}=\left\{a({\rm Im}(\tau),iq)\mid a\in\mathbb{C}\right\}

and

T^{\mathbb{R}}_{q}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}=T_{q}^{H}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}\oplus\{t(0,i)\mid t\in\mathbb{R}\}.

4.4.1.
Sign of the Levi form

For making a conjectural picture of the structure of the sphere bundle, we continue to discuss with this simplest case (cf.
Conjecture

11.1.1
).
The complex Hessian (Levi matrix) of the
L^{1}
-norm function
is

{\rm Levi}(\boldsymbol{n})|_{q}=\begin{bmatrix}0&-i\dfrac{q}{2|q|}\\
i\dfrac{\overline{q}}{2|q|}&\dfrac{{\rm Im}(\tau)}{2|q|}\end{bmatrix}=\dfrac{1}{2|q|}\begin{bmatrix}0&-iq\\
i\overline{q}&{\rm Im}(\tau)\end{bmatrix}.

This defines the Hermitian form on
T_{q}\mathcal{Q}_{1}
. Notice that

\displaystyle(0,i){\rm Levi}(\boldsymbol{n})|_{q}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-i\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{{\rm Im}(\tau)}{2|q|}>0

\displaystyle({\rm Im}(\tau),iq){\rm Levi}(\boldsymbol{n})|_{q}\begin{pmatrix}{\rm Im}(\tau)\\
-i\overline{q}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{|q|}{2}{\rm Im}(\tau)<0.

Thus, the Levi form of
\boldsymbol{n}
is negative on the horizontal subspace
T^{H}_{q}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}
and positive on the vertical space.
We notice that the eigenvalues of the Levi form is
\lambda_{\pm}=({\rm Im}(\tau)\pm\sqrt{{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2}+4|q|^{2}})/2
, and the corresponding eigenspaces are spanned by

V_{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}-2iq\\
{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2}\pm 4|q|^{2}\end{pmatrix}.

In particular, the eigenspace for the negative eigenvalue is not a subspace in the real tangent space to the unit sphere bundle.

On the other hand,
the complex Hessian (Levi matrix) of the Teichmüller metric
\boldsymbol{\tau}
is

{\rm Levi}(\boldsymbol{\tau})|_{(\tau,\mu)}=\begin{bmatrix}\dfrac{|\mu|}{4{\rm Im}(\tau)^{3}}&\dfrac{i\mu}{8|\mu|{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2}}\\
-\dfrac{i\overline{\mu}}{8|\mu|{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2}}&\dfrac{1}{8|\mu|{\rm Im}(\tau)}\end{bmatrix}

which is positive definite. Hence, the Teichmüller metric is strictly plurisubharmonic on
T\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\times}=T\mathcal{T}_{1}-\{0\}
, and the unit sphere bundle
\mathcal{S}T\mathcal{T}_{1}
is pseudoconvex at every point.

4.4.2.
CR structures on the unit tangent bundles

If we restrict the differential of the Teichmüller Beltrami map
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}
to the horizontal subspace
T^{H}_{q}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}
,

D\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}|_{q_{0}}\left({\rm Im}(\tau)\partial_{\tau}+iq\partial q\right)={\rm Im}(\tau)\partial_{\tau}+(-4i\overline{q}{\rm Im}(\tau)^{2})\partial_{\mu}.

This means that the restriction of
D\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}|_{q_{0}}
is
\mathbb{C}
-linear (compare with
Proposition

12.3.1
), and the image of
T^{H}_{q}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}
coincides with the maximal complex subspace in the real tangent space
T^{\mathbb{R}}_{\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}(q)}\mathcal{S}T\mathcal{T}_{1}
of the unit sphere bundle with respect to the Teichmüller metric.
Thus, the subbundle

\cup_{q\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}}T^{H}_{q}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}

defines an (abstract) CR structure on
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{1}
and the Teichmüller Beltrami map
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}
is a CR-isomorphism between unit sphere bundles (cf.
[
13
, §9]
). See
Problem

1
in §
12.3
.

Chapter 5
Models of Spaces and Pairings

5.1.
Model of second order infinitesimal spaces

One of the purpose of this paper is to give models of second order infinitesimal spaces of the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{g}
in view of the theory of moduli of Riemanns surfaces.
To this end, we first formulate the model of the second order infinitesimal spaces.

Let
M
be a complex manifold of dimension
n
.
Let
p\in M
.
In this paper,
a
model of the second order infinitesimal spaces of
M
at
p\in M
consisting of

•

collections of complex vector spaces of dimension
2n
:

\{V_{v}\}_{v\in T_{p}M},\{V^{*}_{v}\}_{v\in T_{p}M},\{W_{\omega}\}_{\omega\in T^{*}_{p}M},\{W^{*}_{\omega}\}_{\omega\in T^{*}_{p}M},

where
V_{v}^{*}
and
W_{\omega}^{*}
are dual spaces of
V_{v}
and
W_{\omega}
, respectively;

•

cor
\omega\in T^{*}_{p}M
, each
W_{\omega}
admits a complex symplectic form

\mathbf{\Omega}_{\omega}
;

•

maps : the filp
A_{p}\colon V_{p}\to V_{p}
, the switch
B_{p}\colon W_{p}\to V^{*}_{p}
, and the dualization
C_{\omega}\colon W_{\omega}\to W^{*}_{\omega}
,
where

\displaystyle V_{p}

\displaystyle=\{(v,X)\mid v\in T_{p}M,X\in V_{v}\},

\displaystyle V^{*}_{p}

\displaystyle=\{(v,Z)\mid v\in T_{p}M,Z\in V^{*}_{v}\},

\displaystyle W_{p}

\displaystyle=\{(\omega,\Omega)\mid\omega\in T^{*}_{p}M,\Omega\in W_{\omega}\};

•

Commutative diagrams. In each diagram, horizontal lines are all exact. :

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>T_{p}M@>{{\mathbf{vi}}_{\scriptscriptstyle M:v}}>{}>T_{v}TM@>{D\Pi_{M}}>{}>T_{p}M@>{}>{}>0\\
\Big\|@V{\Phi_{v}}V{}V\Big\|\\
0@>{}>{}>T_{p}M@>{vi_{v}}>{}>V_{v}@>{Dpr_{v}}>{}>T_{p}M@>{}>{}>0\end{CD}

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>T^{*}_{p}M@>{(\Pi^{\dagger}_{M})^{*}}>{}>T^{*}_{v}\!TM@>{{\mathbf{vp}}_{\scriptscriptstyle M:v}}>{}>T^{*}_{p}M@>{}>{}>0\\
\Big\|@V{\Phi^{\dagger}_{v}}V{}V\Big\|\\
0@>{}>{}>T^{*}_{p}M@>{pr^{*}_{v}}>{}>V^{*}_{v}@>{vp_{v}}>{}>T^{*}_{p}M@>{}>{}>0\end{CD}

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>T_{p}^{*}M@>{\mathbf{vi}^{\dagger}_{\scriptscriptstyle M:\omega}}>{}>T_{\omega}T^{*}\!M@>{D\Pi^{\dagger}_{M}}>{}>T_{p}M@>{}>{}>0\\
\Big\|@V{\Psi_{\omega}}V{}V\Big\|\\
0@>{}>{}>T^{*}_{p}M@>{vi^{\dagger}_{\omega}}>{}>W_{\omega}@>{Dpr^{\dagger}_{\omega}}>{}>T_{p}M@>{}>{}>0\end{CD}

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>T_{p}^{*}M@>{(\Pi^{\dagger}_{M})^{*}}>{}>T^{*}_{\omega}T^{*}\!M@>{{\mathbf{vp}^{\dagger}}_{\scriptscriptstyle M:\omega}}>{}>T_{p}M@>{}>{}>0\\
\Big\|@V{\Psi^{\dagger}_{\omega}}V{}V\Big\|\\
0@>{}>{}>T^{*}_{p}M@>{pr^{\dagger,*}_{\omega}}>{}>W^{*}_{\omega}@>{vp^{\dagger}_{\omega}}>{}>T_{p}M@>{}>{}>0\end{CD}

(Notice that in each commutative diagram, the vertical map in the middle is (automatically) an isomorphism).

with the properties that

(1)

\Phi^{\dagger}_{v}
and
\Psi^{\dagger}_{\omega}
are duals of
\Phi_{v}
and
\Psi_{\omega}
, respectively. Namely,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{TM}(V_{1},V_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{V_{v}}(\Phi_{v}(V_{1}),\Phi^{\dagger}_{v}(V_{2}))

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{T^{*}\!M}(\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{W_{\omega}}(\Psi_{\omega}(\Omega_{1}),\Psi^{\dagger}_{\omega}(\Omega_{2}))

for
V_{1}\in T_{v}TM
,
V_{2}\in T^{*}_{v}\!TM
,
\Omega_{1}\in T_{\omega}T^{*}\!M
and
\Omega_{2}\in T^{*}_{\omega}\!T^{*}\!M
,
where
\mathcal{P}_{V_{v}}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}_{W_{\omega}}
) is the dual pairing between
V_{v}
and
V^{*}_{v}
(resp.
W_{\omega}
and
W^{*}_{\omega}
);

(2)

A_{p}\circ A_{p}=id_{V_{p}}
;

(3)

for
V\in V_{v}
. if
u=Dpr_{v}(V)
,
A_{p}(V)\in V_{u}
and
Dpr_{u}\circ A_{p}(V)=v
;

(4)

let
v\in T_{p}M
and
V\in V_{v}
. Set
u=Dpr_{v}(V)
. For any surjective
\mathbb{C}
-linear mappings
L_{u}\colon V_{u}\to T_{p}M
and
L_{v}\colon V_{v}\to T_{p}M
with
L_{u}\circ vi_{u}=L_{v}\circ vi_{v}=id_{T_{p}M}
,
L_{u}\circ A_{p}(V)=L_{v}(V)
;

(5)

when
p
varies, the derivative of the pairing function
\mathcal{P}_{M}
on
TM\oplus T^{*}\!M
in the direction
(V_{1},V_{2})\in T_{v}TTM\oplus T_{\omega}T^{*}\!M
at
(v,\omega)\in TM\oplus T^{*}\!M
satisfies

D\mathcal{P}_{M}|_{(v,\omega)}[V_{1},V_{2}]=\mathcal{P}_{V_{u}}(A_{p}(V_{1}),B_{p}(V_{2}))

where
u=Dpr_{v}(V_{1})=Dpr^{\dagger}_{\omega}(V_{2})
; and

(6)

for
\Omega_{1}
,
\Omega_{2}\in T_{\omega}T^{*}\!M
,

\mathcal{P}_{W_{\omega}}(\Psi_{\omega}(\Omega_{1}),C_{\omega}(\Psi_{\omega}(\Omega_{2})))=2\mathbf{\Omega}_{\omega}(\Psi_{\omega}(\Omega_{2}),\Psi_{\omega}(\Omega_{1})).

In our case,

M=\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
we will denote the model spaces by

\displaystyle V_{v}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]=\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]=\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{[\nu]}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle V^{*}_{v}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle W_{\omega}

\displaystyle={\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})

\displaystyle W^{*}_{\omega}

\displaystyle={\bf H}^{1,\dagger}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})

for
p=x_{0}=(M_{0},f_{0})\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
,
v=[Y]=[\nu]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})=T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
,
\omega=q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
and an appropriate covering
\mathcal{U}
of
M_{0}
,
defined at
Definition

3
,

Definition

6
,

Definition

7
,
§
5.5
,
§
5.6
,
and
§
5.7

later.
Models of pairings

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{TT}}

\displaystyle\colon\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]\times\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]\to\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\colon{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})\times{\bf H}^{1,\dagger}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})\to\mathbb{C}

are defined in §
5.5.1
and §
5.7.2
. The model of the holomorphic symplectic form

\omega_{\mathcal{Q}_{g}}\colon{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})\times{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})\to\mathbb{C}

is given in §
5.7.3
by reformulating Kawai’s symplectic form (
[

]
) in our setting.

5.2.
Lie bracket and Lie derivative

Let
(U,z)
be an analytic coordinate chart of
M_{0}
and
p,q\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
For
\xi\in H^{0}(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,p}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
,
\eta\in H^{0}(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,q}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
, we define the
Lie bracket
of
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
by

(5.2.1)

[\xi,\eta]=\left(\xi(z)\eta_{z}(z)-\xi_{z}(z)\eta(z)\right)d\overline{z}^{p+q}\otimes\partial_{z}\in H^{0}(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,p+q}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))

(cf.
[
43
, p.265]
).
For
\xi
,
\eta\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
, we can see that

\overline{\partial}[\xi,\eta]=[\overline{\partial}\xi,\eta]+[\xi,\overline{\partial}\eta]\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}})).

For
p\geq 0
and
r,s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
we define the
Lie derivative

L_{\cdot}(\cdot)\colon\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,r}(\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes p}))\oplus\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,s}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\to\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,r+s}(\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes p}))

by

(5.2.2)

L_{\xi}(\omega)(z)dz^{p}d\overline{z}^{r+s}=(\xi(z)\omega_{z}(z)+p\,\xi_{z}(z)\omega(z))dz^{p}d\overline{z}^{r+s},

where
\omega=\omega(z)dz^{p}d\overline{z}^{r}
and
\xi=\xi(z)\dfrac{d\overline{z}^{s}}{dz}
.
We often use the following formula in the following argument:
For
Q\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes 2}))

and
\xi
,
\eta\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M_{0}}))
,

(5.2.3)

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}L_{\xi}(Q)=L_{\overline{\partial}\xi}(Q)+L_{\xi}(\overline{\partial}Q)

(5.2.4)

\displaystyle L_{\xi}(\eta Q)=L_{\eta}(\xi Q)=\partial(\xi\eta Q)

(5.2.5)

\displaystyle L_{\xi}(\eta Q)-\xi L_{\eta}(Q)+[\xi,\eta]Q=0

(5.2.6)

\displaystyle L_{\xi}(L_{\eta}(Q))-L_{\eta}(L_{\xi}(Q))=L_{[\xi,\eta]}(Q).

Convention 3
.

As discussed in
Convention

2
,
for
\mu\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M}))
and
Q\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M}^{\otimes 2}))
, we set

\mu Q=\mu(z)Q(z)d\overline{z}\wedge dz.

For
\xi,\eta\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M}))
,
\mu\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M}))
and
Q\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M}^{\otimes 2}))
,

\xi Q=\xi(z)Q(z)dz\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M}))
and

\xi\mu Q=\mu\xi Q=\xi(z)\mu(z)Q(z)d\overline{z}\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{A}^{0,1}).

Hence

(5.2.7)

\displaystyle d(\xi Q)=\overline{\partial}(\xi Q)=(\xi_{\overline{z}}(z)Q(z)+\xi Q_{\overline{z}}(z))d\overline{z}\wedge dz=(\overline{\partial}\xi)Q+\xi(\overline{\partial}Q)

(5.2.8)

\displaystyle d(\xi\mu Q)=\partial(\xi\mu Q)=(\xi_{z}\mu Q+\xi\mu_{z}Q+\xi\mu Q_{z})dz\wedge d\overline{z}=-L_{\xi}(\mu Q)

(5.2.9)

\displaystyle L_{\xi}(\mu Q)-\mu L_{\xi}(Q)+[\mu,\xi]Q=0

5.3.
Model spaces of
T_{[Y]}T\mathcal{T}_{g}

Let
M_{0}
be a closed Riemann surface of genus
g
.
In this section, we fix a locally finite covering
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
M_{0}
.

5.3.1.
Operators

For
X=\{X_{ij}\}_{i,j\in I}
,
Y=\{Y_{ij}\}_{i,j\in I}\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,
we define (the bilinarization of) the
primary obstruction

\zeta(X,Y)\in Z^{2}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
for
X
and
Y
by

\zeta(X,Y)_{ijk}=\dfrac{1}{2}([X_{ij},Y_{jk}]+[Y_{ij},X_{jk}])

on
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\cap U_{k}
(cf.
[
43
, §5.1]
).
By definition,

\zeta(X,Y)=\zeta(Y,X)

for
X
,
Y\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
We also define

\displaystyle S\colon C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\oplus Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\to C^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})

\displaystyle K,K^{\prime}\colon C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\oplus Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\to C^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})

by

\displaystyle S(\xi,Y)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}[\xi_{i}+\xi_{j},Y_{ij}]

\displaystyle K(\alpha,Y)_{ij}

\displaystyle=[\alpha_{i},Y_{ij}]

\displaystyle K^{\prime}(\alpha,Y)_{ij}

\displaystyle=[\alpha_{j},Y_{ij}].

Then, for
\alpha\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,

(5.3.1)

\displaystyle\delta(S(\alpha,Y))

\displaystyle=\zeta(\delta\alpha,Y)=\zeta(Y,\delta\alpha)

(5.3.2)

\displaystyle K(\alpha,Y)

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{1}{2}[\partial\alpha,Y]+S(\alpha,Y).

(5.3.3)

\displaystyle K^{\prime}(\alpha,Y)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}[\partial\alpha,Y]+S(\alpha,Y).

(cf.
[
43
, §5.1]
). For
X=\{X_{ij}\}_{i,j\in I}\in C^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,
we set

(5.3.4)

X^{*}=\{X_{ji}\}_{i,j\in I}.

5.3.2.
Model spaces
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
for
Y\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})

Henceforth, we fix a
1
-cocycle
Y=\{Y_{ij}\}\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
We consider the following
\mathbb{C}
-linear maps

(5.3.5)

\displaystyle D_{0}^{Y}\colon C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})^{\oplus 2}\to Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\oplus C^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})

(5.3.6)

\displaystyle D_{1}^{Y}\colon Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\oplus C^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\to C^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\oplus C^{2}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})

defined by

(5.3.7)

\displaystyle D^{Y}_{0}(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=(\delta\alpha,\delta\beta+K(\alpha,Y))

(5.3.8)

\displaystyle D^{Y}_{1}(X,\dot{Y})

\displaystyle=\left(\dot{Y}+\dot{Y}^{*}+[X,Y],\delta\left(\dot{Y}+\dfrac{1}{2}[X,Y]\right)-\zeta(X,Y)\right),

where
\alpha=\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}
,
\beta=\{\beta_{i}\}_{i}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,
X=\{X_{ij}\}
,
Y=\{Y_{ij}\}\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,
\dot{Y}=\{\dot{Y}_{ij}\}_{i,j}\in C^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
and

[X,Y]=\{[X_{ij},Y_{ij}]\}_{i,j}\in C^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}}).

We claim

Lemma 5.3.1
.

D^{Y}_{1}\circ D^{Y}_{0}=0
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha=\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}
,
\beta=\{\beta_{i}\}_{i}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
The
(i,j)
-component of the first coordinate of
D^{Y}_{1}\circ D^{Y}_{0}(\alpha,\beta)

satisifes

(\beta_{j}-\beta_{i})+[\alpha_{i},Y_{ij}]+(\beta_{i}-\beta_{j})+[\alpha_{j},Y_{ji}]+[\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{i},Y_{ij}]=0.

From (b) of
Proposition

6.1.1
and (
5.3.1
),
the
(i,j,k)
-component of the second coordinate of
D^{Y}_{1}\circ D^{Y}_{0}(\alpha,\beta)
satisfies

\displaystyle[\alpha_{i},Y_{ij}]+[\alpha_{j},Y_{jk}]+[\alpha_{k},Y_{ki}]

\displaystyle+\dfrac{1}{2}[\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{i},Y_{ij}]+\dfrac{1}{2}[\alpha_{k}-\alpha_{j},Y_{jk}]+\dfrac{1}{2}[\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{k},Y_{ki}]-\zeta(\delta\alpha,Y)

\displaystyle=S(\alpha,Y)_{ij}+S(\alpha,Y)_{jk}+S(\alpha,Y)_{ki}-\delta(S(\alpha,Y))_{ijk}=0

which implies what we wanted.
∎

Definition 1

(Model spaces for cocycles)
.

The
model space of the double tangent space for a cocycle
Y\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
by

\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]=\ker(D^{Y}_{1})/{\rm Im}(D^{Y}_{0}).

We denote by
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}
the equivalence class of
(X,\dot{Y})\in\ker(D^{Y}_{1})
in
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
.

5.3.3.
Vertical spaces in the model space

We discuss the vertical spaces in the model space.

Definition 2

(Vertical spaces)
.

The
vertical spaces

\mathbb{T}^{V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]

in
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]

is defined by

(5.3.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]=\left\{\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{Y}\mid\mbox{$[X]=0$ in $H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})$}\right\}

Proposition 5.3.1

(Vertical space)
.

The verical space
\mathbb{T}^{V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
is canonically isomorphic to
H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
Indeed,

(5.3.10)

\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}\colon\mathbb{T}^{V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]\ni\left\lsem\delta\alpha,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\mapsto[\dot{Y}-K(\alpha,Y)]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})

is a well-defined
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphism, where
\alpha\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.

Proof.

We will show that
Let
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}^{V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
. Let
(X,\dot{Y})\in\ker(D^{Y}_{1})
be a representative of
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}
. By definition,
X=\delta\alpha
for some
\alpha\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
. Hence,

0=\delta(\dot{Y}+\dfrac{1}{2}[X,Y])-\zeta(X,Y)=\delta(\dot{Y}-K(\alpha,Y))

and

\displaystyle(\dot{Y}-K(\alpha,Y))_{ji}

\displaystyle=\dot{Y}_{ji}-[\alpha_{j},Y_{ji}]=-\dot{Y}_{ij}-[\delta\alpha,Y_{ij}]+[\alpha_{j},Y_{ij}]

\displaystyle=-(\dot{Y}_{ij}-[\alpha_{i},Y_{ij}])=-(\dot{Y}-K(\alpha,Y))_{ij}.

Hence,
\dot{Y}-K(\alpha,Y)\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.

Let
(\delta\alpha^{\prime},\dot{Y}^{\prime})\in\ker(D^{Y}_{1})
be another representative of
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
.
Since
(\delta\alpha-\delta\alpha^{\prime},\dot{Y}-\dot{Y}^{\prime})
is trivial in
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
, from the definition of the model space,
there are
\beta
,
\gamma\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
such that

\delta\alpha-\delta\alpha^{\prime}=\delta\gamma
and

\dot{Y}-\dot{Y}^{\prime}=\delta\beta+K(\gamma,Y)
.
Since
M_{0}
admits no non-trivial holomorphic vector field,
\gamma=\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}
. Hence
\dot{Y}-\dot{Y}^{\prime}=\delta\beta+K(\alpha-\alpha^{\prime},Y)
, which is equivalent to

(\dot{Y}-K(\alpha,Y))-(\dot{Y}^{\prime}-K(\alpha^{\prime},Y))=\delta\beta.

This means that the map (
5.3.10
) is well-defined.

Suppose
\left\lsem\delta\alpha,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}
is in the kernel of
\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}
. Then,
\dot{Y}-K(\alpha,Y)=\delta\beta
for some
\beta
, and

(\delta\alpha,\dot{Y})=(\delta\alpha,\delta\beta+K(\alpha,Y))=D_{0}^{Y}(\alpha,\beta).

Therefore
\left\lsem\delta\alpha,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}=0
in
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
. Hence
\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}
is injective.

For
[W]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,
(0,W)\in\ker(D^{Y}_{1})
and
\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}(\left\lsem 0,W\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}})=[W]
.
Therefore,
\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphism.
∎

5.3.4.
Model space
\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]
for
[Y]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})

We shall check the following.

Proposition 5.3.2
.

For
\beta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,
a linear isomorphism on
Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\oplus C^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
defined by

(5.3.11)

\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta;Y}(X,\dot{Y})=(X,\dot{Y}+K^{\prime}(\beta,X))

descends to an isomorphism
\mathcal{L}_{\beta;Y}
from
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
and
\mathbb{T}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]
. Furthermore,
the isomorphism
\mathcal{L}_{\beta;Y}
maps
\mathbb{T}^{V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
onto
\mathbb{T}^{V}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]
.

Proof.

From the definition, if
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta;Y}
descends to the linear map from
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
and
\mathbb{T}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]
, the linear map sends the vertical space in
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
to that in
\mathbb{T}_{Y+\delta}[\mathcal{U}]
. Hence, we only check the descendant of
\tilde{L}_{\beta;Y}
.

Let
(X,\dot{Y})\in\ker(D_{1}^{Y})
.
Then,

\displaystyle(\dot{Y}_{ij}+[\beta_{j},X_{ij}])+(\dot{Y}_{ji}+[\beta_{i},X_{ji}])+[X_{ij},Y_{ij}+\delta\beta]

\displaystyle=\dot{Y}_{ij}+[\beta_{j},X_{ij}]+\dot{Y}_{ji}-[\beta_{i},X_{ij}]+[X_{ij},Y_{ij}]+[X_{ij},\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}]

\displaystyle=\dot{Y}_{ij}+\dot{Y}_{ji}+[X_{ij},Y_{ij}]=0

for
i
,
j\in I
.
From (
5.3.1
), we have

\displaystyle\delta\left(\dfrac{1}{2}[X,\delta\beta]\right)-\zeta(X,\delta\beta)

\displaystyle=\delta\left(\dfrac{1}{2}[X,\delta\beta]-S(\beta,X)\right)=-\delta\left(\dfrac{1}{2}[\delta\beta,X]+S(\beta,X)\right)

\displaystyle=-\delta K^{\prime}(\beta,Y).

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\delta\left(\dot{Y}+K^{\prime}(\beta,Y)+\dfrac{1}{2}[X,Y+\delta\beta]\right)-\zeta(X,Y+\delta\beta)

\displaystyle=\delta\left(\dot{Y}+\dfrac{1}{2}[X,Y]\right)-\zeta(X,Y)=0.

Therefore,
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta;Y}(X,\dot{Y})\in\ker(D_{1}^{Y+\delta\beta})
.

Assume
(X,\dot{Y})\in{\rm Im}(D_{0}^{Y})
.
Then,
X=\delta\alpha
and
\dot{Y}=\delta\gamma+K(\alpha,Y)
for some
\alpha
,
\gamma\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
Since

\displaystyle(\dot{Y}+K^{\prime}(\beta,X))_{ij}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}+[\alpha_{i},Y_{ij}]+[\beta_{j},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{i}]

\displaystyle=\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}+[\alpha_{i},Y_{ij}+\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}]+[\beta_{j},\alpha_{j}]-[\beta_{i},\alpha_{i}]

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{j}+[\beta_{j},\alpha_{j}])-(\gamma_{i}+[\beta_{i},\alpha_{i}])+[\alpha_{i},Y_{ij}+\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}].

we have

\dot{Y}+K^{\prime}(\beta,Y)=\delta(\gamma+[\beta,\alpha])+K(\alpha,Y+\delta\beta),

which means
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta;Y}(X,\dot{Y})\in{\rm Im}(D_{0}^{Y+\delta\beta})
.
Therefore, the map (
5.3.11
) descends to a
\mathbb{C}
-linear map
\mathcal{L}_{\beta;Y}
from
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]\to\mathbb{T}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]
. Since
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{-\beta;Y}\circ\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta;Y}=id
,
\mathcal{L}_{\beta;Y}
is isomorphic.
∎

Definition 3

(Model space and Vertical space for cohomology classes)
.

For
\beta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
, we identify
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
and
\mathbb{T}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]
by
\mathcal{L}_{\beta;Y}
. This identification is well-defined since
\mathcal{L}_{\beta^{\prime};Y+\delta\beta}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\beta;Y}=\mathcal{L}_{\beta^{\prime}+\beta;Y}
for
\beta
,
\beta^{\prime}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
This procedure makes a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space
\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]
.
We call the space
\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]
the
model space of the double tangent space at
[Y]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
. We also define the
vertical space

\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}^{V}[\mathcal{U}]
in the same manner.

We denote by
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\in\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]
the equivalence class of
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
.
In §
8.2
,
we will show that
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
and
\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]
are naturally identified with the tangent space to the tangent bundle over
\mathcal{T}_{g}
at the tangent vector
[Y]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.

Remark 5.3.1

(Sum on the model)
.

From the definition, for the additive operation on
\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]
,

a\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}+b\left\lsem X^{\prime},\dot{Y}^{\prime}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}=\left\lsem Z,\dot{W}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}

if
Z=aX+bX^{\prime}
and
\dot{W}=a\dot{Y}+b\dot{Y}^{\prime}
, where
a
,
b\in\mathbb{C}
,
(X,\dot{Y})
,
(X^{\prime},\dot{Y}^{\prime})\in\ker(D^{Y}_{1})

are representatives of
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}
and
\left\lsem X^{\prime},\dot{Y}^{\prime}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}
for fixed
Y\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
, respectively.

5.3.5.
Vertical space and Cohomology group

Since

\displaystyle\left((\dot{Y}+K^{\prime}(\beta,\delta\alpha)-K(\alpha,Y+\delta\beta)\right)_{ij}

\displaystyle=\dot{Y}+[\beta_{j},\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{i}]-[\alpha_{i},Y]-[\alpha_{i},\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}]

\displaystyle=(\dot{Y}-K(\alpha,Y))+\delta(\{[\beta_{i},\alpha_{i}]\}_{i\in I})

for
\alpha
,
\beta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,
from
Propositions

5.3.1
and
5.3.2
, we obtain a
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphism

(5.3.12)

\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}\colon\mathbb{T}^{V}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]\ni\left\lsem\delta\alpha,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\mapsto[\dot{Y}-K(\alpha,Y)]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})

which satisfies the following commutative diagram:

(5.3.13)

\begin{CD}\mathbb{T}^{V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]@>{\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}>{}>H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\\
@V{\cong}V{}V\Big\|\\
\mathbb{T}^{V}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]@>{}>{\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}>H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}}),\end{CD}

where the left vertical arrow is the natural identification in
Definition

3
.
The inverse of the map
\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}
is

(5.3.14)

\begin{CD}H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})@>{}>{}>\mathbb{T}^{V}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]\\
[\dot{Y}]@>{}>{}>\left\lsem 0,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}.\end{CD}

5.4.
Dolbeaut type presentation of Double tangent spaces

Throughout this section, we assume that a locally finite covering
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
satisfies
H^{1}(U_{i},\Theta_{M_{0}})=0
for all
i\in I
. In this case

\begin{CD}C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))@>{-\overline{\partial}}>{}>C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\end{CD}

is surjective (cf.
[
43
, Theorem 3.13]
).
This section deals with two presentations of the double tangent spaces by complex
(-1,1)
-forms.
The first presentation is defined with respecting to the cohomology presentations of the basepoint. The second is defined with the presentation by the Beltrami differential of the basepoint. The first presentation is a mediator for connecting between the space
\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]
and the second presentation.

5.4.1.
Dolbeault type presentations for cocycles

In the following diagram, we abbreviate
C^{k}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{S})
,

Z^{k}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{S})
,

\Theta_{M_{0}}
and
\mathcal{A}^{p,q}(\Theta_{M_{0}})

to

C^{k}(\mathscr{S})
,

Z^{k}(\mathscr{S})
,

\Theta
and
\mathcal{A}^{p,q}_{\Theta}

for simplicity.
The exact sequence (
3.3.1
)
leads the following commutative diagram:

\begin{CD}C^{0}(\Theta)^{\oplus 2}@>{\iota\oplus\iota}>{}>C^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{0,0}_{\Theta})^{\oplus 2}@>{(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})}>{}>C^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{0,1}_{\Theta})^{\oplus 2}\\
@V{D^{Y}_{0}}V{}V@V{D^{Y;0}_{0}}V{}V@V{D^{Y;1}_{0}}V{}V\\
Z^{1}(\Theta)\oplus C^{1}(\Theta)@>{\iota\oplus\iota}>{}>Z^{1}(\mathcal{A}^{0,0}_{\Theta})\oplus C^{1}(\mathcal{A}^{0,0}_{\Theta})@>{(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})}>{}>Z^{1}(\mathcal{A}^{0,1}_{\Theta})\oplus C^{1}(\mathcal{A}^{0,1}_{\Theta})\\
@V{D^{Y}_{1}}V{}V@V{D^{Y;0}_{1}}V{}V\\
C^{1}(\Theta)\oplus C^{2}(\Theta)@>{\iota\oplus\iota}>{}>C^{1}(\mathcal{A}^{0,0}_{\Theta})\oplus C^{2}(\mathcal{A}^{0,0}_{\Theta}),\end{CD}

where the
\mathbb{C}
-linear maps
D^{Y;0}_{0}
,
D^{Y;0}_{1}
, and
D^{Y;1}_{0}
are natural extensions of
D^{Y}_{0}
and
D^{Y}_{1}
(defined in (
5.3.7
) and (
5.3.8
)) with notation in §
5.2
:

(5.4.1)

\displaystyle D^{Y;0}_{0}(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=(\delta\alpha,\delta\beta+K(\alpha,Y))

(5.4.2)

\displaystyle D^{Y;0}_{1}(\xi,\dot{\eta})

\displaystyle=\left(\dot{\eta}+\dot{\eta}^{*}+[\xi,Y],\delta\left(\dot{\eta}+\dfrac{1}{2}[\xi,Y]\right)-\zeta(\xi,Y)\right),

where
\alpha
,
\beta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
,
\xi\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
and
\dot{\eta}\in C^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
.
Since the map
(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})
at the right in the first line of the diagram is surjective, we have an exact sequence

(5.4.3)

\begin{CD}\ker(D^{Y;0}_{0})@>{(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})}>{}>\ker(D^{Y;1}_{0})\\
@>{\mathscr{D}_{0}}>{}>\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]@>{\iota\oplus\iota}>{}>\ker(D^{Y;0}_{1})/{\rm Im}(D^{Y;0}_{0}),\end{CD}

where
\mathscr{D}_{0}
is the connecting homomorphism.
We claim

Lemma 5.4.1
.

\ker(D^{Y;0}_{1})/{\rm Im}(D^{Y;0}_{0})=\{0\}
.

Proof.

Let
(\Xi,H)\in\ker(D^{Y;0}_{1})
. By definition,

\displaystyle H_{ij}+H_{ji}+[\Xi_{ij},Y_{ij}]=0

\displaystyle\delta\left(H+\dfrac{1}{2}[\Xi,Y]\right)-\zeta(\Xi,Y)=0

for
i
,
j\in I
.
Let
\xi\in C^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{0,0}_{\Theta})
with
\delta\xi=\Xi
.
Since
\zeta(\Xi,Y)=\delta(S(\xi,Y))
,
from (
5.3.1
) and (
5.3.2
),
the above two equations imply that
H-K(\xi,Y)\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{A}^{0,0}_{\Theta})
.
Take
\xi^{\prime}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{A}^{0,0}_{\Theta})
with
\delta\xi^{\prime}=H-K(\xi,Y)
. Then,

D^{Y;0}_{0}(\xi,\xi^{\prime})=(\delta\xi,\delta\xi^{\prime}+K(\xi,Y))=(\Xi,H),

which means
(\Xi,H)\in{\rm Im}(D^{Y;0}_{0})
.
∎

Definition 4

(Dolbeault type presentation for cocycle)
.

We call the quotient space

(5.4.4)

\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]=\ker(D^{Y;1}_{0})/(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})(\ker(D^{Y;0}_{0})).

the
Dolbeault type presentation of the model space for cocycle
Y
.

Remark 5.4.1
.

Though the connecting homomorphism
\mathscr{D}_{0}
might not be defined for arbitrary coverings, we also define

\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]=\ker(D^{Y;1}_{0})/(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})(\ker(D^{Y;0}_{0}))

for an (arbitrary) locally finite covering on
M_{0}
.

From (
5.4.3
) and
Lemma

5.4.1
,
we obtain the following
(cf. (
3.3.2
)).

Theorem 5.4.1

(Dolbeault type presentation of model space)
.

Under the above notation,
the connecting homomorphism
\mathscr{D}_{0}

leads the isomorphism
from
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
onto
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
.

For
(\mu,\dot{\nu})\in\ker(D^{Y;1}_{0})
, we denote by
\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}
the equivalence class of
(\mu,\dot{\nu})
in
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
.

For the record, we notice the following:

•

The kernels
\ker(D^{\prime\prime}_{1})
and
\ker(D^{\prime}_{0})
are

\displaystyle\ker(D^{Y;1}_{0})

\displaystyle=\{(\mu,\dot{\nu})\in H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\oplus C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\mid\delta\dot{\nu}+[\mu,Y]=0\}

\displaystyle\ker(D^{Y;0}_{0})

\displaystyle=\{(\xi,\dot{\eta})\in H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\oplus C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\mid\delta\dot{\eta}+[\xi,Y]=0\},

where
[\mu,Y]=\{[\mu,Y_{ij}]\}_{i,j\in I}
and
[\xi,Y]=\{[\xi_{i},Y_{ij}]\}_{i,j\in I}
.

•

\mathscr{D}_{0}(\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}})=\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}
if and only if there are
\xi
,
\dot{\eta}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
such that

\displaystyle(\mu,\dot{\nu})

\displaystyle=(-\overline{\partial}\xi,-\overline{\partial}\dot{\eta})

\displaystyle(X,\dot{Y})

\displaystyle=(\delta\xi,\delta\dot{\eta}+K(\xi,Y)).

We notice the following.

Proposition 5.4.1
.

Fix
\eta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\eta=Y
.
For
(\mu_{1},\dot{\nu}_{1})
,
(\mu_{2},\dot{\nu}_{2})\in\ker(D^{Y;1}_{0})
,
(\mu_{1},\dot{\nu}_{1})
and
(\mu_{2},\dot{\nu}_{2})
are equivalent in the quotient space
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
if and only if there is
\alpha
,
\beta\in\Gamma(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
such that

(1)

\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}-\overline{\partial}\alpha
; and

(2)

(\dot{\nu}_{1})_{i}=(\dot{\nu}_{2})_{i}+\overline{\partial}[\alpha,\eta_{i}]+\overline{\partial}\beta
on
U_{i}
for
i\in I
.

Proof.

Suppose (1) and (2) hold. Since

([\alpha,\eta_{j}]+\beta)-([\alpha,\eta_{i}]+\beta)+[-\alpha,Y_{ij}]=0

on
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
,

(-\alpha,\{[\alpha,\eta_{i}]+\beta\}_{i\in I})\in\ker(D^{Y;0}_{0})
. Therefore,
(\mu_{1},\dot{\nu}_{1})
and
(\mu_{2},\dot{\nu}_{2})
are equivalent in
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
.

Suppose
(\mu_{1},\dot{\nu}_{1})
and
(\mu_{2},\dot{\nu}_{2})
are equivalent in
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
. Then, there are
\alpha\in\Gamma(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
and
\gamma\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
such that
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}-\overline{\partial}\alpha
,
\dot{\nu}_{1}=\dot{\nu}_{2}-\overline{\partial}\gamma
and

(5.4.5)

\delta\gamma+K(\alpha,Y)=0.

Since
\delta\eta=Y
,

0=\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}+[\alpha,\eta_{j}]-[\alpha,\eta_{i}]

on
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
.
This means that

\beta=-\gamma_{i}-[\alpha,\eta_{i}]

on
U_{i}
becomes a global smooth vector field on
M_{0}
and satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}\beta

\displaystyle=(\dot{\nu}_{1})_{i}-(\dot{\nu}_{2})_{i}-\overline{\partial}[\alpha,\eta_{i}]

on
U_{i}
.
∎

5.4.2.
Vertical spaces in the Dolbeaut type presentation for cocycle

We discuss the Dolbeaut type presentation of the vertical space of the model space.

Definition 5

(Vertical spaces in the Dolbeaut type presentation)
.

The
vertical spaces

\mathbb{T}^{Dol,V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
in
\mathbb{T}_{Y}^{Dol}[\mathcal{U}]
is defined by

(5.4.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{Dol,V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]=\{\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}\mid\mbox{$[\mu]=0$ in $T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}$}\}.

Proposition 5.4.2

(Connecting isomorphism and vertical spaces)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a locally finite covering of
M_{0}
with
H^{1}(U_{i},\Theta_{M_{0}})=0
for
i\in I
.
Then, the connecting isomorphism

\mathscr{D}_{0}\colon\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]\to\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]

induces an isomorphism between the vertical spaces.

Proof.

Let
\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
. Suppose
\mathscr{D}_{0}(\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}})=\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{Y}
.
By the definition of the connecting homomorphism, there are
\alpha
,
\beta\in C^{0}(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
such that
\partial\alpha_{i}=-\mu
,
\overline{\partial}\beta_{i}=-\dot{\nu}_{i}
for
i\in I
,
\delta\alpha=X
and
\delta\beta+K(\alpha,Y)=\dot{Y}
.

Assume first that
\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Dol,V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
.
Since
[\mu]=0
, there is a section
\gamma\in\Gamma(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
such that
\overline{\partial}\gamma=-\mu
. We define
\xi\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
by
\xi_{i}=\alpha_{i}-\gamma
on
U_{i}
for
i\in I
. Then,
\delta\xi=X
, and hence
[X]=0
. This means that
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}^{V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
.

Conversely, suppose
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}^{V}_{Y}
. Then, there is
\xi\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
such that
\delta\xi=X
.Hence,
\gamma=\alpha-\xi
defines a global smooth vector field on
M_{0}
with
\overline{\partial}\gamma=-\mu
. This means that
[\mu]=0
and
\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Dol,V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
.

Thus we conclude that
\mathscr{D}_{0}(\mathbb{T}^{Dol,V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}])=\mathbb{T}^{V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
, and the vertical spaces are isomorphic via the connecting isomorphism
\mathscr{D}_{0}
.
∎

5.4.3.
Dolbeault type presentations for cohomology classes

We start with the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4.2
.

For
\beta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,
a linear isomorphism on
C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}_{\Theta})^{\oplus 2}
defined by

(5.4.7)

\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}(\mu,\dot{\nu})=(\mu,\dot{\nu}-[\mu,\beta])

descends to an isomorphism
\mathcal{L}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}
from
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
to
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]
.
Furthermore,
if a locally finite covering
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
M_{0}
satisfies
H^{1}(U_{i},\Theta_{M_{0}})=0
for
i\in I
. Then,

\mathcal{L}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}
preserves the vertical spaces, and satisfies the following commutative diagram:

(5.4.8)

\begin{CD}\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]@>{\mathscr{D}_{0}}>{}>\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]\\
@V{\mathcal{L}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}}V{}V@V{}V{\mathcal{L}_{\beta;Y}}V\\
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]@>{\mathscr{D}_{0}}>{}>\mathbb{T}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]\\
\end{CD}

Proof.

First we check
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}
descends to a map from
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
to
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]
.
Let
(\mu,\dot{\nu})\in\ker(D^{Y;1}_{0})
and
(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\dot{\nu}})=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}(\mu,\dot{\nu})
. Then,

\displaystyle\tilde{\dot{\nu}}_{j}-\tilde{\dot{\nu}}_{i}+[\tilde{\mu},Y_{ij}+\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}]

\displaystyle=(\dot{\nu}_{j}+\partial[\xi_{j},\beta_{j}])-(\dot{\nu}_{i}+\partial[\xi_{i},\beta_{i}])+[\mu,Y_{ij}]+[\mu,\beta_{j}]-[\mu,\beta_{i}]

\displaystyle=-[\mu,\beta_{j}]+[\mu,\beta_{i}]+[\mu,\beta_{j}]-[\mu,\beta_{i}]=0.

Hence,
(\tilde{\mu},\tilde{\dot{\nu}})\in\ker(D^{Y+\delta\beta;1}_{0})
.

Suppose that
(\mu,\dot{\nu})\in{\rm Im}((-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial}))
. There are
\xi\in H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
and
\eta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
such that
\mu_{i}=-\overline{\partial}\xi_{i}
,
\dot{\nu}_{i}=-\overline{\partial}\dot{\eta}_{i}
and

\dot{\eta}_{j}-\dot{\eta}_{i}+[\xi,Y_{ij}]=0

for
i
,
j\in I
.
Let
\tilde{\xi}=\xi
and
\tilde{\eta}=\dot{\eta}-[\xi,\beta]
. Then

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}_{j}-\tilde{\eta}_{i}+[\tilde{\xi},Y_{ij}+\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}]

\displaystyle=(\dot{\eta}_{j}-[\xi_{j},\beta_{j}])-(\dot{\eta}_{i}-[\xi_{i},\beta_{i}])+[\xi,Y_{ij}+\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}]=0

\displaystyle(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})(\tilde{\xi},\tilde{\eta})_{i}

\displaystyle=(-\overline{\partial}\tilde{\xi}_{i},-\overline{\partial}\tilde{\eta}_{i})=(-\overline{\partial}\xi_{i},-\overline{\partial}\dot{\eta}_{i}-[-\overline{\partial}\xi_{i},\beta_{i}])

\displaystyle=(\mu_{i},\dot{\nu}_{i}-[\mu,\beta_{i}])=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}(\mu,\dot{\nu})_{i}

for
i\in I
.
Hence

\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}(\mu,\dot{\nu})\in{\rm Im}((-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial}))
. Since
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{Dol}_{-\beta;Y+\delta\beta}\circ\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}
is the identity map,
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}
descends a
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphism

\mathcal{L}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}\colon\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]\to\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]
.

Next, we check the diagram (
5.4.8
) is commute in the case where
H^{1}(U_{i},\Theta_{M_{0}})=0
for
i\in I
.
Let
(\mu,\dot{\nu})\in\ker(D^{Y;1}_{0})
, and
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}=\mathscr{D}_{0}(\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}})
. Then, there are
\xi
,
\dot{\eta}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}})
such that

\displaystyle(\mu_{i},\dot{\nu}_{i})

\displaystyle=(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})(\xi,\dot{\eta})_{i}=(-\overline{\partial}\xi_{i},-\overline{\partial}\dot{\eta}_{i})

\displaystyle X_{ij}

\displaystyle=\xi_{j}-\xi_{i}

\displaystyle\dot{Y}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\dot{\eta}_{j}-\dot{\eta}_{i}+[\xi_{i},Y_{ij}]

for
i
,
j\in I
.
Let
\tilde{\xi}=\xi
and
\tilde{\eta}=\dot{\eta}-[\xi,\beta]
. Then,
for
\beta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,

\displaystyle(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})(\tilde{\xi},\tilde{\eta})_{i}

\displaystyle=(-\overline{\partial}\tilde{\xi}_{i},-\overline{\partial}\tilde{\eta}_{i})=(\mu_{i},\dot{\nu}_{i}-[\mu_{i},\beta_{i}])=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}(\mu,\dot{\nu})_{i}

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}_{j}-\tilde{\xi}_{i}

\displaystyle=\xi_{j}-\xi_{i}=X_{ij}

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}_{j}-\tilde{\eta}_{i}+[\tilde{\xi}_{i},Y_{ij}+\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}]

\displaystyle=(\dot{\eta}_{j}-[\xi_{j},\beta_{j}])-(\dot{\eta}_{i}-[\xi_{i},\beta_{i}])+[\xi_{i},Y_{ij}+\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}]

\displaystyle=\dot{\eta}_{j}-\dot{\eta}_{i}+[\xi_{i},Y_{ij}]-[\xi_{j},\beta_{j}]+[\xi_{i},\beta_{j}]

\displaystyle=\dot{Y}_{ij}+K^{\prime}(\beta,X)_{ij}.

This implies that the connecting homomorphism
\mathscr{D}_{0}
sends
the equivalence class of
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}(\mu,\dot{\nu})
in

\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]

to that of
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\beta;Y}(X,\dot{Y})
in
\mathbb{T}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]
. This means that the diagram (
5.4.8
) is commute.
From
Proposition

5.4.2
, the isomorphism
\mathcal{L}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}
preserves the vertical spaces.
∎

Definition 6

(Model space and Vertical space for cohomology classes)
.

For
\beta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
, we identify
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
and
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]
by
\mathcal{L}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}
.
The identified
\mathbb{C}
-vector space
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]
is called the
Dolbeault type presentation of the model space of the double tangent space at
[Y]\in H^{1}(M_{0},\Theta_{M_{0}})\cong\mathcal{T}_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
. We also define the
vertical space

\mathbb{T}^{Dol,V}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]
in the same manner.

From
Lemma

5.4.2
, we have

Corollary 5.4.1
.

The connecting homomorphism
\mathscr{D}_{0}
induces an isomorphism

\mathscr{D}_{0}\colon\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]\to\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]

which respects the vertical spaces.

5.4.4.
Vertical spaces and Tangent spaces

Fix
\eta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\eta=Y
.
From
Proposition

5.4.1
and
Lemma

5.4.2
,

(5.4.9)

\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}\colon\mathbb{T}^{Dol,V}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]\ni\left\langlebar-\overline{\partial}\alpha,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\mapsto[\{\dot{\nu}_{i}-\overline{\partial}[\alpha,\eta_{i}]\}_{i\in I}]\in T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}

is a well-defined
\mathbb{C}
-linear mapping which satisfies the following diagram:

(5.4.10)

\begin{CD}\mathbb{T}^{Dol,V}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]@>{\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}>{}>T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}\\
@V{\cong}V{}V\Big\|\\
\mathbb{T}^{Dol,V}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]@>{}>{\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}>T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g},\end{CD}

since
\delta(\eta+\beta)=Y+\delta\beta
for
\beta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
The inverse of the map
\mathbf{vi}^{\mathbb{T}}_{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}
is

(5.4.11)

\begin{CD}T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}@>{}>{}>\mathbb{T}^{V}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]\\
[\dot{\nu}]@>{}>{}>\left\langlebar 0,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}.\end{CD}

5.4.5.
Dolbeaut type presentation in terms of Beltrami differentials

In this section, we discuss the presentation of the double tangent space in terms of Beltrami differentials.
The construction is almost same as that of the Dolbeaut presentation for cohomology classes.

Let
[Y]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
For
\eta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\eta=Y
, we set

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle=\{(\mu,\dot{\nu})\in H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\oplus C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\mid\delta(\dot{\nu}+[\mu,\eta])=0\}

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle=\{(\xi,\zeta)\in H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\oplus C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\mid\delta(\zeta+[\xi,\eta])=0\},

where
[\mu,\eta]=\{[\mu,\eta_{i}]\}_{i\in I}
and
[\xi,\eta]=\{[\xi,\eta_{i}]\}_{i\in I}
.
We notice that as subspaces of
H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\oplus C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))

and
H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))\oplus C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
,
the above spaces
\mathbb{Z}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]
and
\mathbb{B}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]
coincide with the spaces
\ker(D^{Y;1}_{0})
and
\ker(D^{Y;0}_{0})
defined in §
5.4.1
, respectively.

The linear map

(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})\colon\mathbb{B}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]\to\mathbb{Z}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]

is well-defined.
Indeed,
for
(\xi,\zeta)\in\mathbb{B}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]
,
let

(\mu,\dot{\nu})=(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})(\xi,\zeta)=(-\overline{\partial}\xi,-\overline{\partial}\zeta)
.
Then,

\displaystyle\delta(\dot{\nu}+[\mu,\eta])_{ij}

\displaystyle=(\dot{\nu}_{j}+[\mu,\eta_{j}])-(\dot{\nu}_{i}+[\mu,\eta_{i}])

\displaystyle=-\overline{\partial}(\xi_{j}-\xi_{i})-[\overline{\partial}\xi,\eta_{j}-\eta_{i}])=-\overline{\partial}\left((\xi_{j}-\xi_{i})+[\xi,Y_{ij}]\right)

\displaystyle=-\overline{\partial}\left((\xi_{j}+[\xi,\eta_{j}]-(\xi_{i}+[\xi,\eta_{i}])\right)=0

for
i
,
j\in I
.
We define

(5.4.12)

\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]=\mathbb{Z}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]/(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})(\mathbb{B}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]).

We denote by
\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\eta}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]
the equivalence class of
(\mu,\dot{\nu})\in\mathbb{Z}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]
.
By definition,
there is a canonical identification

(5.4.13)

\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]\ni\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\mapsto\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\eta}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]

since
\delta\eta=Y
.

The presentation
\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]
depends on the choice of
\eta
with
\delta\eta=Y
.
Indeed, let
\eta^{\prime}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))

with
\delta\eta^{\prime}=Y
. Then, the following diagram is commutative:

\begin{CD}\mathbb{B}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]@>{(\xi,\zeta)\mapsto(\xi,\zeta+[\xi,\eta-\eta^{\prime}])}>{}>\mathbb{B}^{Bel}_{\eta^{\prime}}[\mathcal{U}]\\
@V{(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})}V{}V@V{}V{(-\overline{\partial})\oplus(-\overline{\partial})}V\\
\mathbb{Z}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]@>{(\xi,\zeta)\mapsto(\mu,\dot{\nu}+[\mu,\eta-\eta^{\prime}])}>{}>\mathbb{Z}^{Bel}_{\eta^{\prime}}[\mathcal{U}].\end{CD}

In the diagram, we notice that
\eta-\eta^{\prime}
defines a global vector field on
M_{0}

and
\overline{\partial}(\delta\eta)=\overline{\partial}(\delta\eta^{\prime})=0
. Therefore,
we obtain a canonical isomorphism

(5.4.14)

\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]\ni\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\eta}}\mapsto\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}+[\mu,\eta-\eta^{\prime}]\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\eta^{\prime}}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\eta^{\prime}}[\mathcal{U}].

By applying the above discussion, for
\beta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
, we can check that the linear map

\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{Dol}_{\beta;Y}
on
C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}_{\Theta})^{\oplus 2}
defined in (
5.4.7
) sends
\mathbb{Z}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]

onto
\mathbb{Z}^{Bel}_{\eta+\beta}[\mathcal{U}]
, and descend to an isomorphism

(5.4.15)

\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]\ni\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\eta}}\mapsto\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}-[\mu,\beta]\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\eta+\beta}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\eta+\beta}[\mathcal{U}].

Definition 7

(Dolbeaut presentation in terms of Beltrami differentials)
.

Let
[\nu]\in T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
. We define the
Dolbeaut presentation of the model space in terms of Beltrami differentials

\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{[\nu]}[\mathcal{U}]
as the isomorphism class of the vector spaces
\{\mathbb{T}_{\eta}[\mathcal{[}\mathcal{U}]\}_{\eta}

via (
5.4.14
) and (
5.4.15
), where
\eta
runs all cochain
\eta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
[-\overline{\partial}\eta]=[\nu]
.
For
\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\eta}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]
,
we denote by
\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle[\nu]}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{[\nu]}[\mathcal{U}]

the equivalence class of
\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\eta}}
.

Let
[Y]\in H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
with
\mathscr{T}_{x_{0}}([Y])=[\nu]
.
We notice the following.

•

Two spaces
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]
and
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{[\nu]}[\mathcal{U}]
are naturally isomorphic by a canonical identification (
5.4.13
).

•

The connecting homomorphism
\mathscr{D}_{0}\colon\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]\to\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]
discussed in
Proposition

5.4.2
is naturally thought of as an isomorphism

\mathscr{D}_{0}\colon\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]\to\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]

when
\eta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
satisfies
\delta\eta=Y
.

•

The vertical space in
\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{[\nu]}[\mathcal{U}]
is also described by the same argument as that in §
5.4.2
and (
5.4.9
).

5.5.
Model of
T^{*}_{[Y]}T\mathcal{T}_{g}
and Model of the pairing

Let
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a locally finite covering on
M_{0}
.
Let
x_{0}=(M_{0},f_{0})\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
Y\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
We define the linear maps

\displaystyle D^{Y,\dagger}_{1}\colon C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes 2})^{\oplus 2}\to C^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes 2})^{\oplus 2}

by

D^{Y,\dagger}_{1}(\psi,q)=(\delta\psi-L_{Y}(q),\delta q)

where
L_{Y}(q)_{ij}=L_{Y_{ij}}(q_{0})
for
i
,
j\in I
.
We call

\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]=\ker(D^{Y,\dagger}_{1})

the
model space of the cotangent space to the tangent bundle for a cocycle
Y\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
. Notice that the inclusion on the first coordinate

T^{*}_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}=\mathcal{Q}_{M_{0}}=H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes 2})\ni\psi\mapsto(\psi,0)\in\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]

is well-defined. We call this map the
horizontal inclusion
. The projection, which we call the
vertical projection
,
on the second coordinate

\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]\ni(\psi,q)\mapsto q\in\mathcal{Q}_{M_{0}}=T^{*}_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}

leads the identification between the cokernel of the horizontal inclusion and
\mathcal{Q}_{M_{0}}
. Thus, we have the following exact sequence

(5.5.1)

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes 2})@>{}>{}>\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]@>{}>{}>H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes 2})@>{}>{}>0\\
\psi @>{}>{}>(\psi,0)\\
(\psi,q)@>{}>{}>q\end{CD}

In particular,
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]=6g-6
when
H^{1}(U_{i},\Theta_{M_{0}})=0
for
i\in I
(cf.
Theorem

6.4.1
later).

To define the
model space

\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]

of the cotangent space to the tangent bundle for
[Y]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,
we identify
two spaces
\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
and
\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}]
for
\beta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
via the
\mathbb{C}
-linear isomorphism

(5.5.2)

\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\beta;Y}\colon\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]\ni(\psi,q)\to(\psi+L_{\beta}(q),q)\in\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{Y+\delta\beta}[\mathcal{U}].

We denote by
\left\lsem\psi,q\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\in\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]
the corresponding element to
(\psi,q)\in\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
. From the definition,
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\beta;Y}
commutes the horizontal inclusion. Thus, the diagram (
5.5.1
) leads the following exact sequence:

(5.5.3)

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes 2})@>{}>{}>\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]@>{}>{}>H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes 2})@>{}>{}>0\\
\psi @>{}>{}>\left\lsem\psi,0\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\ \\
\left\lsem\psi,q\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\ @>{}>{}>q.\end{CD}

5.5.1.
Model of Pairing between
TT\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
T^{*}T\mathcal{T}_{g}

Let
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a locally finite covering.
Let
Y\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
To define the
pairing
between
\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
and
\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
, we start with the following lemma.

Lemma 5.5.1
.

For
(X,\dot{Y})\in\ker(D^{Y}_{1})
,
(\psi,q)\in\ker(D^{Y,\dagger}_{1})
, and
\xi\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\xi=X
,
we define

\displaystyle\omega_{ij}

\displaystyle=X_{ij}\psi_{i}-\dot{Y}_{ji}q+L_{Y_{ij}}(\xi_{j}q)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X_{ij}\psi_{i}+(\dot{Y}_{ij}+[X_{ij},Y_{ij}])q+L_{Y_{ij}}(\xi_{j}q)}.

Then,
\{\omega_{ij}\}_{i,j\in I}\in C^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M_{0}}))
satisfies the following

(1)

\omega=\{\omega_{ij}\}_{i,j\in I}\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M_{0}}))
.

(2)

Let
A=\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta A=\omega
, and
\eta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\eta=Y
.
Then,
\overline{\partial}A-L_{\eta}(d(\xi q))=\{(A_{i})_{\overline{z}}-L_{\eta_{i}}((\xi_{i})_{\overline{z}}q)\}_{i\in I}
defines an area element on
M_{0}
.

(3)

Under the notation in (2), the integral

(5.5.4)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathbb{TT}}((X,\dot{Y}),(\psi,q))

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{1}{2i}\iint_{M_{0}}((A_{i})_{\overline{z}}-L_{\eta_{i}}((\xi_{i})_{\overline{z}}q))d\overline{z}\wedge dz

is independent of the choice of the representatives
Y
of
[Y]
,
(X,\dot{Y})
in
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}
and
(\psi,q)\in\ker(D^{Y,\dagger}_{1})
in
\left\lsem\psi,q\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}
.

Proof.

(1)
From the argument in the proof of
Proposition

5.3.1
and (
5.2.5
),

\displaystyle(\delta\omega)_{ijk}

\displaystyle=(X_{ij}\psi_{i}-\dot{Y}_{ji}q+L_{Y_{ij}}(\xi_{j}q))+(X_{jk}\psi_{j}-\dot{Y}_{kj}q+L_{Y_{jk}}(\xi_{j}q))

\displaystyle\quad+(X_{ki}\psi_{k}-\dot{Y}_{ik}q+L_{Y_{ki}}(\xi_{i}q))

\displaystyle=\xi_{i}(\psi_{k}-\psi_{i})+\xi_{j}(\psi_{i}-\psi_{j})+\xi_{k}(\psi_{j}-\psi_{k})

\displaystyle\quad-([\xi_{j},Y_{ji}]+[\xi_{k},Y_{kj}]+[\xi_{i},Y_{ik}])q

\displaystyle\qquad+L_{Y_{ij}}(\xi_{j}q)+L_{Y_{jk}}(\xi_{j}q)+L_{Y_{ki}}(\xi_{k}q)

\displaystyle=\xi_{i}L_{Y_{ik}}(q)+\xi_{j}L_{Y_{ji}}(q)+\xi_{k}L_{Y_{kj}}(q)

\displaystyle\quad-([\xi_{j},Y_{ji}]+[\xi_{k},Y_{kj}]+[\xi_{i},Y_{ik}])q

\displaystyle\qquad-L_{Y_{ji}}(\xi_{j}q)-L_{Y_{kj}}(\xi_{k}q)-L_{Y_{ik}}(\xi_{i}q)=0

for
i
,
j
,
k\in I
.

(2) The existence of such an
A=\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
follows from the fact that the sheaf
\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M_{0}})
is fine (e.g.
[
43
, §3.4]
).
Since
(\xi_{j})_{\overline{z}}=(\xi_{i})_{\overline{z}}
on
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
,
for
z\in z_{j}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})
,

\displaystyle(A_{j})_{\overline{z}}(z)-(A_{i})_{\overline{z}}(z_{ij}(z))\left|\dfrac{dz_{ij}}{dz}(z)\right|^{2}=\left(A_{j}(z)-A_{i}(z_{ij}(z))\dfrac{dz_{ij}}{dz}(z)\right)_{\overline{z}}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(X_{ij}(z)\psi_{i}(z_{ij}(z))-\dot{Y}_{ji}(z)q_{i}(z_{ij}(z))+L_{Y_{ij}}(\xi_{j}q_{j})(z_{ij}(z))\right)\dfrac{dz_{ij}}{dz}(z))\right)_{\overline{z}}

\displaystyle=L_{Y_{ij}}((\xi_{j})_{\overline{z}}q_{j})(z_{ij}(z))\left|\dfrac{dz_{ij}}{dz}(z)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=L_{\eta_{j}}((\xi_{j})_{\overline{z}}q_{j})(z)-L_{\eta_{i}}((\xi_{i})_{\overline{z}}q_{i})(z_{ij}(z)))\left|\dfrac{dz_{ij}}{dz}(z)\right|^{2},

where
q=\{q_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes 2})=\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
.
This implies what we wanted.

(3) We will show that the integral is independent of the choices of

(i)

A\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta A=\omega
;

(ii)

\eta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
