The purpose of this chapter is to define the models of the flip, the switch, and the dualizaiton for the models of the second order infinitesimal spaces.
We also give a presentation of the Lie bracket for
C^{1}
-vector fields of type
(1,0)
on the Teichmüller space.

10.1.
Model spaces over underlying manifolds

For a complex manifold
M
, the flip on
TTM
, the switch from
TT^{*}\!M
to
T^{*}TM
, and the dualization between
T^{*}\!T^{*}\!M
and
TT^{*}\!M
preserves the fibers over points of
M
. From this observation, we define

\displaystyle(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle=\left\{\left([Y],\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\right)\mid[Y]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}}),\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\in\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]\right\}

\displaystyle(\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}\mathbb{T})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle=\left\{\left([Y],\left\lsem\psi,q\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\right)\mid[Y]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}}),\left\lsem\psi,q\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\in\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]\right\}

\displaystyle(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{\dagger})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(q,[X,\varphi]_{q}\right)\mid q\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}},[X,\varphi]_{q}\in{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q})\right\}

\displaystyle(\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}\mathbb{T}^{\dagger})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(q,[\Phi,Y]_{q}\right)\mid q\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}},[\Phi,Y]_{q}\in{\bf H}^{1,\dagger}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q})\right\}.

where
x_{0}=(M_{0},f_{0})\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
\mathcal{U}
be a locally finite covering of
M_{0}

with
H^{1}(U_{i},\Theta_{M_{0}})=0
for
i\in I
.

Throughtout this chapter, we fix a guiding frame
\mathfrak{gf}
and the good section
\mathfrak{L}
for trivializations (cf. §
6.1
and
Theorem

6.2.1
).
From
Theorem

6.2.1
,
Theorem

6.3.1
, and
Theorem

6.5.1
, we have the following bijections:

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\colon(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]\to H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})^{\oplus 3}

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\colon(\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}\mathbb{T})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]\to H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\colon(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{\dagger})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]\to\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}^{*\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\colon(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{\dagger})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]\to\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})

by

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}_{\mathbb{T}}([Y],\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}})

\displaystyle=\left([Y],\mathscr{I}_{[Y]}\left(\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}([Y],\left\lsem\psi,q\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}

\displaystyle=\left([Y],\mathscr{I}^{\dagger}_{[Y]}\left(\left\lsem\psi,q\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(q,[X,\varphi]_{q})

\displaystyle=\left(q,\mathscr{I}^{*}_{q}\left([X,\varphi]_{q}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\mathscr{I}^{*\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}(q,[\Phi,Y]_{q})

\displaystyle=\left(q,\mathscr{I}^{*\dagger}_{q}\left([\Phi,Y]_{q}\right)\right).

10.2.
Flip

In this section, we discuss the flip on the double tangent space.

10.2.1.
Action on the model spaces

We define

\displaystyle(\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{Z})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(Y,(X,\dot{Y})\right)\mid Y\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}}),(X,\dot{Y})\in\ker(D^{Y}_{1})\right\},

where
D^{Y}_{1}
is a linear map defined in §
5.3.2
.
We claim

Proposition 10.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a locally finite covering of
M_{0}
.
We define
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{Z},x_{0}}\colon(\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{Z})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]\to(\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{Z})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{T}}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{T},x_{0}}\colon(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]\to(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]
by

(10.2.1)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{Z},x_{0}}\left(Y,(X,\dot{Y})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(X,(Y,\dot{Y}+[X,Y])\right)

(10.2.2)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{T},x_{0}}\left([Y],\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left([X],\left\lsem Y,\dot{Y}+[X,Y]\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[X]}}\right).

Then,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{Z},x_{0}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{T},x_{0}}
are well-defined self-maps on
(\mathbb{ZZ})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]
and
(\mathbb{TT})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]
satisfying the commutative diagram:

\begin{CD}(\mathbb{ZZ})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]@>{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{Z}}}>{}>(\mathbb{ZZ})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
(\mathbb{ZZ})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]@>{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{T}}}>{}>(\mathbb{TT})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}],\end{CD}

where vertical arrows mean the natural projection.

Proof.

We need to check that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{Z},x_{0}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{T},x_{0}}
are well-defined.

Let
(X,\dot{Y})\in\ker(D^{Y}_{1})
. For
i
,
j\in I
,

\displaystyle(\dot{Y}_{ij}+[X_{ij},Y_{ij}])+(\dot{Y}_{ij}+[X_{ij},Y_{ij}])+[Y_{ij},X_{ij}]=\dot{Y}_{ij}+\dot{Y}_{ji}+[X_{ij},Y_{ij}]=0.

\displaystyle\delta\left((\dot{Y}+[X,Y])+\dfrac{1}{2}[Y,X]\right)-\zeta(Y,X)=\delta\left((\dot{Y}+\dfrac{1}{2}[X,Y]\right)-\zeta(X,Y)=0,

which means that
(Y,\dot{Y}+[X,Y])\in\ker(D^{X}_{1})
. Therefore,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{Z},x_{0}}
is well-defined.

Next, we check
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{T},x_{0}}
is well-defined.
For
\alpha
,
\beta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
,
the second cooridnate of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{Z}}(Y,(X,\dot{Y})+D_{0}^{Y}(\alpha,\beta))
is equal to

\displaystyle(Y,\dot{Y}+\delta\beta+K(\alpha,Y)+[X+\delta\alpha,Y])

\displaystyle=(Y,\dot{Y}+[X,Y]+K^{\prime}(\alpha,Y)+(0,\delta\beta)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\alpha;X}(Y,\dot{Y}+[X,Y])+D_{0}^{X+\delta\alpha}(0,\beta).

Since
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\alpha;X}(Y,\dot{Y}+[X,Y])\in\ker(D^{Y+\delta\alpha}_{1})
,
from
Proposition

5.3.2
and
Definition

3
,
the equivalence class
\left\lsem Y,\dot{Y}+[X,Y]\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[X]}}\in\mathbb{T}_{[X]}
is well-defined from
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{Y}
.
Notice that
\tilde{L}_{\beta;Y}(X,\dot{Y})=(X,\dot{Y}+K^{\prime}(\beta,X))

(
\in\ker(D^{Y+\delta\beta}_{1})
) corresponds to

\displaystyle(Y+\delta\beta,\dot{Y}+K^{\prime}(\beta,X)+[X,Y+\delta\beta])

\displaystyle=(Y+\delta\beta,\dot{Y}+[\beta_{i},X]+[X,Y])

\displaystyle=(Y,\dot{Y}+[X,Y])+D_{0}^{X}(\beta,0).

In the sequel, for
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\in\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}
,
the equivalence class
\left\lsem Y,\dot{Y}+[X,Y]\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[X]}}
is well-defined in
\mathbb{T}_{[X]}
.
∎

10.2.2.
The action of the Flip
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}

We define
a self-map
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{triv}_{\mathbb{T},x_{0}}
of
(\mathbb{TT})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]
by

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{triv}_{\mathbb{T},x_{0}}([Y],[X],[W])

\displaystyle=([X],[Y],[W]),

where
\mathscr{I}_{[Y]}
is the trivialization defined in
Theorem

6.2.1
.
We define
\mathscr{K}_{x_{0}}\colon(TT)_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}\to(\mathbb{TT})_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
by

\mathscr{K}_{x_{0}}(v)=\left(\mathscr{T}_{x_{0}}^{-1}(\Pi_{T\mathcal{T}_{g}}(v)),\mathscr{K}_{\Pi_{T\mathcal{T}_{g}}(v)}^{\Pi_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}(\Pi_{T\mathcal{T}_{g}}(v))}(v)\right),

where
\mathscr{K}_{\cdot}^{\cdot}
is the isomorphism defined in
Theorem

8.2.1
.

We discuss the flip on the double tangent space on Teichmüller space. For
x_{0}\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
, let
\mathscr{B}_{x_{0}}\colon\mathcal{T}_{g}\to B_{2}
be the Bers embedding and
\mathcal{T}^{B}_{x_{0}}
the image (§
3.1.3
). Then the twice differentials of the Bers embedding
\mathscr{B}_{x_{0}}
induces the canonical isomorphism

TT\mathcal{T}_{g}\to\mathcal{T}^{B}_{x_{0}}\times B_{2}\times B_{2}\times B_{2}

since
\mathcal{T}^{B}_{x_{0}}
is a domain in
B_{2}
(
\cong\mathbb{C}^{3g-3}
). Under the identification, the action of the flip
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}
on
TT\mathcal{T}_{g}
is presented by

\begin{CD}\mathcal{T}^{B}_{x_{0}}\times B_{2}\times B_{2}\times B_{2}@>{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}}>{}>\mathcal{T}^{B}_{x_{0}}\times B_{2}\times B_{2}\times B_{2}\\
(x,\psi_{0},\dot{\varphi},\dot{\psi})@>{}>{}>(x,\dot{\varphi},\psi_{0},\dot{\psi})\end{CD}

from the definition of the flip (cf. §
2.6.1
).

Our model of the flip (
10.2.2
) presents the action of the flip on the double tangent space. Actually we have the following.

Theorem 10.2.1

(Flip)
.

The flip
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{T}}
on
(\mathbb{TT})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]
satisfying the commutative diagram:

\begin{CD}(TT)_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}@>{\mathscr{K}_{x_{0}}}>{}>(\mathbb{TT})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]@>{\mathscr{I}_{\mathbb{T}}}>{}>H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})^{\oplus 3}\\
@V{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}}V{}V@V{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{T}}}V{}V@V{}V{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{triv}_{\mathbb{T}}}V\\
(TT)_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}@>{\mathscr{K}_{x_{0}}}>{}>(\mathbb{TT})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]@>{\mathscr{I}_{\mathbb{T}}}>{}>H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})^{\oplus 3},\end{CD}

where
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}
is the flip on
TT\mathcal{T}_{g}
.

Proof.

From
Theorem

8.2.1
, the commutativity of the left-square of the diagram follows from that of the right-square by applying a special guiding frame defined in (
6.1.1
). Hence, we only check the commutativity of the right-square.

Let
([X],[W])\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})^{\oplus 2}
and
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
with

\mathscr{I}_{Y}\left(\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\right)=([X],[W])
. Suppose
\mathscr{I}_{X}\left(\left\lsem Y,\dot{Y}+[X,Y]\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\right)=([Y],[W^{\prime}])
. Then

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\dot{Y}+\dfrac{1}{2}[X,Y]-E(X,Y)

\displaystyle W^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\dot{Y}+[X,Y]+\dfrac{1}{2}[Y,X]-E(Y,X)=\dot{Y}+\dfrac{1}{2}[X,Y]-E(Y,X)

modulo
\delta C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
, where
\mathcal{U}
is an appropriate covering on
M
.
From (d) of
Lemma

6.2.1
,

W-W^{\prime}=E(X,Y)-E(Y,X)\equiv 0

modulo
\delta C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
Hence
[W]=[W^{\prime}]
in
H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
∎

10.2.3.
Flip via Dolbeaut presentations

From the (
8.1.11
)
and
Corollary

9.2.1
, it is natural to define, in the cocycle level, the flip under the two Doubeaut-type presentations (given in §
5.4
)
for
\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{Y}^{Dol}[\mathcal{U}]
and
\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\eta}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\eta}^{Bel}[\mathcal{U}]

by

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{Dol}_{\mathbb{T}}(\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}})=\left\langlebar-\overline{\partial}\eta,\dot{\nu}-\overline{\partial}[\xi,\eta]\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\in\mathbb{T}_{X}^{Dol}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{Bel}_{\mathbb{T}}(\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\eta}})=\left\langlebar-\overline{\partial}\eta,\dot{\nu}-\overline{\partial}[\xi,\eta]\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\xi}}\in\mathbb{T}_{\xi}^{Bel}[\mathcal{U}]

for
\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
and
\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}]
, after fixing cochains
\xi
,
\eta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\xi=X
,
-\overline{\partial}\xi=\mu
, and
\delta\eta=Y
, where
[\xi,\eta]=\{[\xi_{i},\eta_{i}]\}_{i\in I}
.

Indeed,
take a cochain
\dot{\eta}
with
-\overline{\partial}\dot{\eta}=\dot{\nu}
.
Let

\dot{Y}=\delta\dot{\eta}+K(\xi,Y)
.
Then

\displaystyle\dot{Y}+[X,Y]

\displaystyle=\delta\dot{\eta}+(K^{\prime}(\xi,Y)-K(\eta,X))+K(\eta,X)

\displaystyle=\delta\dot{\eta}+([\xi_{j},\eta_{j}]-[\xi_{j},\eta_{i}]-[\eta_{i},\xi_{j}]+[\eta_{i},\xi_{i}])+K(\eta,X)

\displaystyle=\delta(\dot{\eta}+[\xi,\eta])+K(\eta,X).

From (
10.2.2
),
we deduce

-\overline{\partial}(\dot{\eta}+[\xi,\eta])=\dot{\nu}-\overline{\partial}[\xi,\eta]

is the second coordinate of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{Dol}_{\mathbb{T}}(\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}})
(and hence, of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}^{Bel}_{\mathbb{T}}(\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\eta}})
).
The commutatice diagrams like as those given in
Proposition

10.2.1
and
Theorem

10.2.1
also hold in the Dolbeaut-type representations from above observations.

10.3.
Dualization, Pairing and Symplectic form

10.3.1.
Trivialization

Let
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a locally finite covering with
H^{1}(U_{i},\Theta_{M_{0}})=0
for
i\in I
.
We define the model of the
dualization
by

{\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}\colon{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})\ni[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}\to[\varphi,-X]_{q_{0}}\in{\bf H}^{1,\dagger}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}}).

We already discussed the model
\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}
of the pairing between
T_{q_{0}}T^{*}\mathcal{T}_{g}\cong{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})
and
T^{*}_{q_{0}}T^{*}\mathcal{T}_{g}\cong{\bf H}^{1,\dagger}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})
, and the model
\omega_{\mathcal{Q}_{g}}
of the symplectic form on
T_{q_{0}}T^{*}\mathcal{T}_{g}\cong{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})
in
Section

5.7
.

We set

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\colon(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{\dagger})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]\to(\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}\mathbb{T}^{\dagger})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}{}^{triv}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\to\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})

by

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}(q,[X,\varphi]_{q})

\displaystyle=(q,[\varphi,-X]_{q})

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}{}^{triv}(q,[X],\varphi)

\displaystyle=(q,\varphi,-[X]).

We can easily check that these maps are well-defined.
From the definitions and
Theorem

6.5.2
, we deduce the following
(cf.
Proposition

2.6.3
).

Theorem 10.3.1

(Dualization, Pairing and Symplectic form)
.

The diagram

\begin{CD}(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{\dagger})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]@>{\mathscr{I}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}}>{}>\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\\
@V{{\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}}V{}V@V{}V{{\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}{}^{triv}}V\\
(\mathbb{T}\dagger\mathbb{T}^{\dagger})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]@>{\mathscr{I}^{*\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}}>{}>\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\end{CD}

is commutative.
Furthermore,

\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}([X,\varphi]_{q},{\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}([X,\varphi^{\prime}]_{q}))=2\omega_{\mathcal{Q}_{g}}([X^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}]_{q},[X,\varphi]_{q})

holds for
[X,\varphi]_{q}
,
[X^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime}]_{q}\in(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{\dagger})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]
.

10.4.
Switch

10.4.1.
Action on the model spaces

We check the following (cf.
Proposition

2.7.1
)

Proposition 10.4.1

(Switch)
.

The mapping

{\bf Swh}_{\mathbb{T}}\colon(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{\dagger})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]\ni(q,[X,\varphi]_{q})\mapsto([X],\left\lsem\varphi,q\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[X]}})\in(\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}\mathbb{T})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]

is a well-defined bijection, and satisfies the following with the differential of the pairing:

(10.4.1)

D\mathcal{P}|_{(v,q)}[V_{1},V_{2}]=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{TT}}|_{[X]}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{T}}(V_{1}),{\bf Swh}_{\mathbb{T}}(V_{2}))

for
V_{1}=\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]\cong T_{[Y]}T\mathcal{T}_{g}
,
V_{2}=[\varphi,X]\in{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})\cong T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
,

[Y]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\cong H^{1}(M_{0},\Theta_{M_{0}})\cong T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
q\in Q_{M_{0}}\cong T^{*}_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
.

Proof.

We only check the well-definedness of the map
{\bf Swh}_{\mathbb{T}}
. The relation (
10.4.1
) follows from
Proposition

9.2.1
.

Let
(X,\varphi)
and
(X^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})
be two representatives of
[X,\varphi]_{q}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q})
.
There is an
\alpha\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
such that
X^{\prime}=X+\delta\alpha
,
\delta\varphi=L_{X}(q)
, and
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi+L_{\alpha}(q)
. Therefore,

(\varphi,q)\in\ker(D^{X,\dagger}_{1})
,

(\varphi^{\prime},q)\in\ker(D^{X^{\prime},\dagger}_{1})
,
and

(\varphi^{\prime},q)=(\varphi+L_{\alpha}(q),q)=\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}_{\alpha;X}(\varphi,q)

and hence
\left\lsem\varphi,q\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[X]}}
is well-defined in
\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{[X]}[\mathcal{U}]
.
The bijectivity follows from the definition.
∎

We define the map
{\bf Swh}^{triv}_{\mathbb{T}}
by

\begin{CD}\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}@>{{\bf Swh}^{triv}_{\mathbb{T}}}>{}>H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\\
(q,[X],\psi)@>{}>{}>([X],\psi,q).\end{CD}

From the definition of the switch, we have the following commutative diagram:

\begin{CD}(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{\dagger})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]@>{\mathscr{I}^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}}>{}>\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\\
@V{{\bf Swh}_{\mathbb{T}}}V{}V@V{}V{{\bf Swh}^{triv}_{\mathbb{T}}}V\\
(\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}\mathbb{T})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]@>{\mathscr{I}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}}}>{}>H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\end{CD},

where the trivializations in the vertical directions of the diagram can be chosen arbitrarily.

10.4.2.
Maps on the second order infinitesimal spaces

As noticed in §
9.3
, our model spaces
(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]
and
(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{*})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]
are thought of as a model of
(TT)_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
(TT^{*})_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
, respectively, via the Bers embedding (or the Ahlfors-Weill section).
We denote by
\mathscr{K}^{\dagger}_{x_{0}}
the natural identification from
(TT^{*})_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
to
(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{*})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]
.
As discussed in §
9.3.2
, there are natural bijections

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}^{*}_{x_{0}}

\displaystyle\colon(T^{*}T)_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}\to(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{T})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}^{*\dagger}_{x_{0}}

\displaystyle\colon(T^{*}T^{*})_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}\to(\mathbb{T}^{*}\mathbb{T}^{*})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]

defined from the dual isomorphisms.

From
Proposition

10.4.1
,
we have the following commutative diagram for the switch between
T^{*}T\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
TT^{*}\mathcal{T}_{g}
:

(10.4.2)

\begin{CD}(T^{*}T)_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}@>{{\bf Swh}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}}>{}>(TT^{*})_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}\\
@V{\cong}V{}V@V{\cong}V{}V\\
(\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}\mathbb{T})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]@>{{\bf Swh}_{\mathbb{T}}}>{}>(\mathbb{T}\mathbb{T}^{\dagger})_{x_{0}}[\mathcal{U}]\end{CD}

where two vertical arrows are canonical identification (cf. §
2.6.2
and §
9.3
).

Remark 10.4.1
.

For the record, we notice the following:
In the correspondence (
10.4.2
) that for
([X],\left\lsem\psi,q\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[X]}})
in the left-hand side, we first choose
X
of a representative of
[X]
and the representaive
(\psi,q)\in\ker(D^{X,\dagger}_{1})
of
\left\lsem\psi,q\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[X]}}
. Then,
(X,\psi)
is a cocycle in
Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q})
.

10.5.
Lie bracket between vector fields

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be
C^{1}
-vector fields of type
(1,0)
defined around
x_{0}
. We think of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
as smooth maps from a neighborhood of
x_{0}
in
\mathcal{T}_{g}
to
T\mathcal{T}_{g}
.

Theorem 10.5.1

(Lie bracket)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a locally finite covering of
M
satisfying that each
U_{i}
is an embedded closed disk with smooth boundary.
Under the above notation,
let
\mathcal{X}_{x_{0}}=[X]
and
\mathcal{Y}_{x_{0}}=[Y]\in T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}\cong H^{1}(M_{0},\Theta_{M_{0}})\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
. Suppose that

\displaystyle\left(D\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{Y})_{[X]}\right)^{10}

\displaystyle=\left\lsem Y,\dot{X}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\in\mathbb{T}_{X}[\mathcal{U}]\cong\mathbb{T}_{[X]}[\mathcal{U}]\cong T_{[X]}T\mathcal{T}_{g}

\displaystyle\left(D\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{X}\right)_{[Y]})^{01}

\displaystyle=\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]\cong\mathbb{T}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]\cong T_{[Y]}T\mathcal{T}_{g}.

Then, the Lie bracket of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
at
x
is represented by

(10.5.1)

\left[\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\right]_{x_{0}}=[\dot{Y}-\dot{X}+[X,Y]]\in H^{1}(M_{0},\Theta_{M_{0}}).

In particular,
\left[\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\right]_{x_{0}}=0
if and only if

\dot{X}-\dot{Y}=[X,Y]+\delta\beta

for some
\beta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.

Proof.

From
Proposition

10.2.1
,

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{Z}}(Y,(X,\dot{Y}))-(X,(Y,\dot{X}))=(X,(0,\dot{Y}-\dot{X}+[X,Y]))

is a lift of

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{\mathbb{T}}\left([Y],\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\right)-\left([X],\left\lsem Y,\dot{X}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[X]}}\right),

and its vertical projection is presented as
[\dot{Y}-\dot{X}+[X,Y]]\in H^{1}(M_{0},\Theta_{M_{0}})
(cf.
Proposition

5.3.1
and
Remark

5.3.1
). The formula (
10.5.1
) follows from
Proposition

2.6.1
.
∎

Remark 10.5.1
.

Since
\dot{Y}^{*}=-\dot{Y}-[X,Y]
, (
10.5.1
) is restated as

(10.5.2)

\left[\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\right]_{x_{0}}=[\dot{Y}^{*}+\dot{X}]\in H^{1}(M_{0},\Theta_{M_{0}})

where
*
means the filp of subscripts defined in (
5.3.4
).

10.6.
Lie bracket via the Dolbeaut presentation

We rephrase
Theorem

10.5.1
under the Dolbeaut presentations.
Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
as above.
Fix cochains
\xi
,
\eta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\xi=X
and
\delta\eta=Y
.
Suppose that

\displaystyle\left(D\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{Y})_{[X]}\right)^{10}

\displaystyle=\left\langlebar-\partial\eta,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle X}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{X}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle=\left\langlebar-\partial\eta,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\xi}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\xi}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle\left(D\mathcal{Y}(\mathcal{X}\right)_{[Y]})^{01}

\displaystyle=\left\langlebar-\partial\xi,\dot{\mu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]

\displaystyle=\left\langlebar-\partial\xi,\dot{\mu}\right\ranglebar_{{\scriptscriptstyle\eta}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Bel}_{\eta}[\mathcal{U}].

From the discussion in §
10.2.3
,
we have

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\right]_{x_{0}}=[\dot{\nu}-\dot{\mu}+\overline{\partial}[\xi,\eta]]\in T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g},

which implies that

\left\langle\left[\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\right]_{x_{0}},q\right\rangle=\iint_{M_{0}}(\dot{\nu}_{i}-\dot{\mu}_{i}+[\xi_{i},\eta_{i}]_{\overline{z}})q

for all
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}=T^{*}_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
.
Thus,
\left[\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\right]_{x_{0}}=0
if and only if
there is a global vector field
\chi\in\Gamma(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
such that

\dot{\nu}-\dot{\mu}+\overline{\partial}[\xi,\eta]=-\overline{\partial}\chi.

Chapter 11
Variational formulae of
L^{1}
-Norm function and Teichmüller metric

11.1.
L^{1}
-norm for holomorphic quadratic differentials

In this section, we discuss the variational formula of the
L^{1}
-norm for holomorphic quadratic differentials

T^{*}\mathcal{T}_{g}\cong\mathcal{Q}_{g}\ni q\mapsto\boldsymbol{n}(q):=\|q\|

on the cotangent bundle. It is known that the
L^{1}
-norm function
\boldsymbol{n}
is of class
C^{1}
on the cotangent bundle except for the zero section (cf.
[
68
, Lemma 2]
).
For reader’s convenience, we summarize Royden’s argument briefly.

Let
x_{0}=(M_{0},f_{0})\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
. Fix a symplectic basis
\{A_{k},B_{k}\}_{k=1}^{g}
of
H_{1}(M_{0},\mathbb{Z})
.
Let
w_{0}
be an Abelian differential on
M_{0}
with simple zeros. For
x=(M,f)\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
, we denote by
w^{x}_{0}
the Abelian differential on
M
with same
A
-periods as
w_{0}
. Take a small neighborhood
U
of
x_{0}
so that each zero of
w^{x}_{0}
for
x\in U
are simple. We label the zeros of
w_{0}^{x}
. Let
w^{x}_{i}
be the normalized Abelian differential on
M
whose
A_{k}
-period is
\delta_{ik}
for
k=1,\cdots,g
. For
i=g+1
,
\cdots
,
3g-3
, let
\gamma_{i}^{\mu}
be the Abelian differential of the third kind which has simple pole of residue
1
at the
(i-g)
-th zero of
w_{0}^{x}
, and a simple pole of residue
-1
at the
2g-2
-nd zero of
w_{0}^{x}
.
Then,

U\times\mathbb{C}\mapsto(x,(t_{1},\cdots,t_{3g-3}))\mapsto\left(\sum_{i=1}^{g}t_{i}w_{i}^{x}+\sum_{i=g+1}^{3g-3}t_{j}\gamma_{j}^{x}\right)w_{0}^{x}\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}

gives a differentiable (holomorphic) local trivialization. We can check that the differential in the right of the above equation is differentiable (cf. Lemma 1 in
[
68
]
). Hence, the norm function varies as a function of
C^{1}
.
We apply (a part of) Royden’s argument to obtain our variational formula and present the variational formula in our own setting.

11.1.1.
Variational formula derived from Royden’s calculation

The calculation here almost follows from that by Royden in
[
68
]
. We start with the following lemma.

Lemma 11.1.1

(Lemma 1 in
[
68
]
)
.

Let
q_{0}
be a holomorphic quadratic diffetential on a Riemann surface
W
, and
\varphi
a smooth (not necessarily holomorphic) quadratic differential. Let
K
be a compact set in
W
. Then the function

f(t)=\iint_{K}|q_{0}(z)+t\varphi(z)|dxdy

is differentiable as a function of
t
, and satisfies

f(t)=f(0)+\dfrac{t}{2}\iint_{K}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}\varphi}{|q_{0}|}dxdy+\dfrac{\overline{t}}{2}\iint_{K}\dfrac{q_{0}\overline{\varphi}}{|q_{0}|}dxdy+o(t).

In the proof of
Lemma

11.1.1
, Royden first discusses the variation

|q_{0}+t\varphi|=|q_{0}|+t\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{2|q_{0}|}\varphi+\overline{t}\dfrac{q_{0}}{2|q_{0}|}\overline{\varphi}+o(t)

on a compact set on which
|q_{0}|>0
, and take the limit via the exhaustion of
K
by relatively compact domains of
K\cap\{|q_{0}|>0\}
.
By applying Royden’s argument and
Proposition

9.1.1
, we also prove the following
(see also the proof of
[
15
, Theorem 5.3]
).

Theorem 11.1.1

(Variation of
L^{1}
-norm)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a locally finite covering of
M_{0}
with
H^{1}(U_{i},\Theta_{M_{0}})=0
for all
i\in I
.
For
V=[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}\in{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})\cong T_{q_{0}}T^{*}\mathcal{T}_{g}
, we have

(11.1.1)

D\boldsymbol{n}|_{q_{0}}[V]=\dfrac{1}{4i}\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\left(\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})\right)d\overline{z}\wedge dz,

where
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
and
\xi=\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{C}^{1,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\xi=X
.

We notice the following.

(1)

Since the zeros of
q_{0}
is discrete, the integral (
11.1.1
) makes sense.

(2)

In the calculation of the formula (
11.1.1
), the following observation may be useful : For a differentiable vector field
X=X(z)\partial_{z}
on
U_{i}
,

L_{X}(|q_{0}|)=\left(\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{2|q_{0}|}(q_{0})^{\prime}X+|q_{0}|X_{z}\right)d\overline{z}\wedge dz=\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{2|q_{0}|}L_{X}(q_{0})

on
z_{i}^{0}(U_{i})\cap\{|q_{0}|>0\}
.

(3)

The formula (
11.1.1
) is independent of the choice of
\xi
. Indeed, take
\Xi=\{\Xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{C}^{1,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\Xi=X
. Then
\mathcal{X}=\xi-\Xi
is a global
\mathscr{C}^{1,1}
-vector field on
M_{0}
. In this case

\displaystyle\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\left(\varphi_{i}-L_{\Xi_{i}}(q_{0})\right)-\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\left(\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}L_{\mathcal{X}}(q_{0})=-2\iint_{M_{0}}d(\mathcal{X}|q_{0}|d\overline{z})=0

by the Green theorem.

(4)

The formula (
11.1.1
) is independent of the choice of the representative of
V=[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}
. Let
\alpha\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
. We now choose
(X+\delta\alpha,\varphi+L_{\alpha}(q_{0}))
as the representative of
V=[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}
. In this case, we may think
\xi+\alpha
instead of
\xi
, and the inside of the parentheses of the integrand in (
11.1.1
) becomes

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}+L_{\alpha_{i}}(q_{0})-L_{\xi_{i}+\alpha_{i}}(q_{0})=\varphi-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0}).

This means that the integral is invariant.

(5)

The formula (
11.1.1
) recovers Royden’s formula in
Lemma

11.1.1
. Indeed, when
[X]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
is trivial, from the above observation, we may assume that
\xi\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
. Hence,
\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})
in the integrand of the right-hand side of (
11.1.1
) is defined from a (global) holomorphic quadratic differential on
M_{0}
.

From the definition of the pairing (
5.7.1
), we conclude the following:

Corollary 11.1.1

(
\partial
-derivative of
L^{1}
-norm)
.

Let
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}
. Then,

\partial\boldsymbol{n}|_{q_{0}}=\dfrac{1}{2}[-L_{\eta}(q_{0}),Y]^{\dagger}_{q_{0}}=-\dfrac{1}{2}[L_{\eta}(q_{0}),-Y]^{\dagger}_{q_{0}}\in{\bf H}^{1,\dagger}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})\cong T^{*}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}

where
L_{\eta}(q_{0})=\{L_{\eta_{i}}(q_{0})\}_{i\in I}
,
\eta_{i}=\{\eta_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{C}^{1,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with

(\eta_{i})_{\overline{z}}=-\overline{q_{0}}/|q_{0}|
for
i\in I

and
Y=\delta\eta\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.

Namely,
Corollary

11.1.1
implies

\partial\boldsymbol{n}([X,\varphi]_{q_{0}})=\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}\left([X,\varphi]_{q_{0}},-\dfrac{1}{2}[L_{\eta}(q_{0}),-Y]^{\dagger}_{q_{0}}\right)

for
[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}\in{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})\cong T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
and
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}
.

Remark 11.1.1
.

Under the notation in the above discussion,
we notice that
L_{\eta_{i}}(q_{0})
is holomorphic on
U_{i}
for all
i\in I
and satisfies

L_{\eta_{j}}(q_{0})-L_{\eta_{i}}(q_{0})=L_{Y_{ij}}(q_{0})
for
i
,
j\in I
. Indeed,

\displaystyle L_{\eta_{i}}(q_{0})_{\overline{z}}

\displaystyle=(q_{0})^{\prime}(\eta_{i})_{\overline{z}}+2q_{0}(\eta_{i})_{\overline{z}z}=-(q_{0})^{\prime}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}+2q_{0}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}^{2}(q_{0})^{\prime}}{2|q_{0}|^{3}}=0

on
U_{i}-{\rm Zero}(q_{0})
.
Since
\overline{q_{0}}/|q_{0}|\in L^{\infty}(U_{i})
, we may think that
\eta_{i}
is Hölder continuous with exponent
1-(2/p)
and
(\eta_{i})_{z}\in L^{p}(U_{i})
for arbitrary
p>2
by applying Calderón-Zygmund’s theorem after identifying
U_{i}
with a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
(e.g.
[
32
, Lemma 4.20, Proposition 4.23]
).
In particular, we have
L_{\eta_{i}}(q_{0})\in L_{loc}^{2}(U_{i})
under the chart of
U_{i}
. Therefore, the Riemann removable singularity theorem (or the Weyl lemma) asserts that
L_{\eta_{i}}(q_{0})
is holomorphic on
U_{i}
.

11.1.2.
Variational formula from the universal deformation

In
[
28
]
, Hubbard and Masur discuss the representation of the tangent vectors at
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}
adapted to the condition of critical points of
q_{0}
. In this section, we restate the differential formula in this setting. The subject of this subsection is not needed for further development in this paper. However, it is interesting by itself.

Let
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{M_{0}}
and
[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}\in{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})\cong T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
, where
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a locally finite covering with
H^{1}(U_{i},\Theta_{M_{0}})=0
for
i\in I
. Let
\xi=\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
with
\delta\xi=X
. We consider the reasonable decomposition
\{R_{j}\}_{j\in J}
of
M_{0}
such that

•

each
R_{j}
contains at most one zero of
q_{0}
in its interior, and
|q_{0}|>0
on
\partial R_{j}
; and

•

each
R_{j}
is contained in
U_{i(j)}
for some
i(j)\in I
.

Let
J
be the set of indices
j\in J
such that the interior of
R_{j}
contains a zero
p_{j}\in M_{0}
of
q_{0}
. For
j\in J\setminus J_{0}
, we fix
p_{j}\in{\rm Int}(R_{j})
. We also assume

•

for
j\in J_{0}
, there is a local chart
(V_{j},w_{j})
around
p_{j}
such that
\overline{R}_{j}\subset V_{j}
,
w_{j}(p_{j})=0
, and
q_{0}=w^{k_{j}}dw^{2}
under the coordinate
(V_{j},w_{j})
.

For
j\in J\setminus J_{0}
, we set
k_{j}=0
.
For
k\geq 0
, we define

P_{k}=\{(a_{0}+a_{1}w+\cdots+a_{k-2}w^{k-2})dw^{2}\mid a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}\}

for
k\geq 2
, and
P_{k}=\{0\}
for
k=0
,
1
.
From the universal deformation theorem by Hubbard and Masur,
each
\varphi_{i(j)}
is represented as

\varphi_{i(j)}=\mathfrak{p}_{\varphi;j}+L_{Z_{j}}(q_{0})

on
R_{j}
,
where
\mathfrak{p}_{\varphi;j}\in P_{k_{j}}
and
Z_{j}
is a (bounded) holomorphic vector field on
V_{j}
(cf.
[
28
, Proposition 3.1]
).

Under the above notation, the formula (
11.1.1
) is rewritten as

(11.1.2)

D\boldsymbol{n}|_{q_{0}}[V]=\dfrac{1}{4i}\sum_{j\in J}\iint_{R_{j}}\left(\dfrac{\overline{w}}{|w|}\right)^{k_{j}}\left(\mathfrak{p}_{\varphi;j}-L_{\xi_{i(j)}-Z_{j}}(w^{k_{j}}dw^{2})\right)d\overline{w}\wedge dw.

11.1.3.
Tangent space to the unit sphere bundle

In this section, we summerize the structure of the real and complex tangent spaces to the unit tangent bundle in
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
.
As Royden observed that the
L^{1}
-norm function is of class
C^{1}
on
\mathcal{Q}^{\times}_{g}=\mathcal{Q}_{g}-\{0\}
(“
0
” means the zero section),
See §
11.2
for detail.

Let

\mathcal{SQ}_{g}=\{q\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}\mid\|q\|=1\}.

The bundle
\mathcal{SQ}_{g}\to\mathcal{T}_{g}
is the unit sphere bundle over
\mathcal{T}_{g}
. Let
x_{0}=(M_{0},f_{0})\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{M_{0}}
. From
Theorem

11.1.1
, the real tangent space to
\mathcal{SQ}_{g}
at
q_{0}
is described by

\displaystyle T^{\mathbb{R}}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{SQ}_{g}

\displaystyle=\left\{V\in T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}\mid{\rm Re}\left(\partial\boldsymbol{n}(V)\right)=0\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}\in T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}\mid{\rm Re}\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{2|q_{0}|}\left(\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})\right)=0\right\},

where

\xi=\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{C}^{1,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}})
with
\delta\xi=X

and
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a locally finite covering with
H^{1}(U_{i},\Theta_{M_{0}})=0
for
i\in I
. In the following argument we identify
\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
with the vertical space of
T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}={\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})
. Namely,

\begin{CD}\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}\ni\psi @>{\cong}>{}>[0,\psi]_{q_{0}}\in{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}}).\end{CD}

(cf.
Remark

5.6.1
). Let

(\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}})^{\perp}_{q_{0}}=\left\{[0,\psi]_{q_{0}}\in{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})\mid\psi\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}},\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\psi=0\right\}.

Take
[X_{q_{0}}]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
such that the tangent vector corresponding to
[X_{q_{0}}]
is presented by
\overline{q_{0}}/|q_{0}|
.
Fix a guiding frame
\mathfrak{gf}
such that
\mathfrak{gf}([X_{q_{0}}])=\overline{q_{0}}/|q_{0}|
.
Take
\mathfrak{L}
as
Proposition

6.1.1
for
\mathfrak{gf}
. In
Proposition

6.1.1
, we assume that all
\mathfrak{gf}([X])
is smooth. However, we can treat this case in similar way.

We define a
horizontal lift
of
[X]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
by

V^{H}([X])=[X,L_{\mathfrak{L}(X)_{i}}(q_{0})-Q_{X}]_{q_{0}}\in T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}={\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}}),

where
Q_{X}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Omega_{M_{0}})^{\otimes 2})
with

\overline{\partial}Q_{X}=\overline{\partial}L_{\mathfrak{L}(X)_{i}}(q_{0}).

and

(11.1.3)

\iint_{M_{0}}\mathfrak{gf}([Z])Q_{X}=0

for all
[Z]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
Notice that the horizontal lift is independent of choice of the representative
X
.
Indeed, for
\alpha\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
, from the definition of
Q_{X}
.
Q_{X+\delta\alpha}=Q_{X}
and

\displaystyle[X+\delta\alpha,L_{\mathfrak{L}(X+\delta\alpha)_{i}}(q_{0})-Q_{X+\delta\alpha}]_{q_{0}}

\displaystyle=[X+\delta\alpha,L_{\mathfrak{L}(X)_{i}+\alpha_{i}}(q_{0})-Q_{X}]_{q_{0}}

\displaystyle=[X+\delta\alpha,L_{\mathfrak{L}(X)_{i}}(q_{0})-Q_{X}+L_{\alpha_{i}}(q_{0})]_{q_{0}}

\displaystyle=[X,L_{\mathfrak{L}(X)_{i}}(q_{0})-Q_{X}]_{q_{0}}.

Therefore,

(11.1.4)

T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}\ni H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\ni[X]\mapsto V^{H}([X])\in T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}

is an injective
\mathbb{C}
-linear map.
We also call the image
T^{H}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
the
horizontal lift of
T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
(with the guiding frame
\mathfrak{gf}
)
.
We claim

Proposition 11.1.1
.

The maximal complex subspace
T^{1,0}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{SQ}_{g}
of
T^{\mathbb{R}}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{SQ}_{g}
satisfies

(11.1.5)

T^{1,0}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{SQ}_{g}=T^{H}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}\oplus(\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}})^{\perp}_{q_{0}}.

In particular,

T^{\mathbb{R}}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{SQ}_{g}=T^{H}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}\oplus(\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}})^{\perp}_{q_{0}}\oplus\langle[0,iq_{0}]_{q_{0}}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}}.

Proof.

The horizontal lift
T^{H}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
and
(\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}})^{\perp}_{q_{0}}
are linearly independent since
(\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}})^{\perp}_{q_{0}}
is contained in the kernel of the differential of the projection
D\Pi_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}^{\dagger}\colon T\mathcal{Q}_{g}\to T\mathcal{T}_{g}
. Therefore, the dimension of the right-hand side of (
11.1.5
) is
3g-3+3g-4=6g-7
.
For a horizontal lift
V=[X,L_{\mathfrak{L}(X)_{i}}(q_{0})-Q]_{q_{0}}\in T^{H}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
, from
Theorem

11.1.1
,

D\boldsymbol{n}|_{q_{0}}[V]=\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{2|q_{0}|}(L_{\mathfrak{L}(X)_{i}}(q_{0})-Q-L_{\mathfrak{L}(X)_{i}}(q_{0}))=-\dfrac{1}{2}\iint_{M_{0}}\mathfrak{gf}([X_{q_{0}}])Q=0.

Therefore,
T^{H}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
is a subspace of
T^{1,0}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{SQ}_{g}
.
Since the dimension of
T^{1,0}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{SQ}_{g}
is
6g-7
, (
11.1.5
) holds. We can easily check the decomposition of
T^{\mathbb{R}}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{SQ}_{g}
.
∎

Remark 11.1.2
.

The horizontal lift
T^{H}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
is dependent of the choice of the guiding frame
\mathfrak{gf}
. In fact, the dependence is caused from an observation that the differential
Q
in the definition of the horizontal lift determined up to
(\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}})^{\perp}_{q_{0}}
when the guiding frame changes.

11.1.4.
Conjectures on Levi convexity of unit ball bundles

Suppose
q_{0}
is generic, that is,
q_{0}
is in the principal stratum.
Under the period coordinates
z=(z_{1},\cdots,z_{6g-6})
via the double (branched) covering around
q_{0}
, the
L^{1}
-norm is presented as

\boldsymbol{n}(z)=i\sum_{j=1}^{3g-3}(z_{j}\overline{z_{g+j}}-z_{g+j}\overline{z_{j}})

after fixing a symplectic basis on the covering surface.
Since the period coordinates via the double covering is a complex analytic chart,
the Levi form of
\boldsymbol{n}
has exactly
3g-3
positive (negative) eigenvalues (cf.
[
51
]
,
[
78
]
. See also
[
15
, §5.4]
).

Recall that a
C^{2}
function on in a complex manifold of dimension
n
is said to be
strictly
k
-pseudoconvex
if its Levi form has at least
n-k+1
positive eigenvalues. A domain
D
in a complex manifold is called
k
-convex
at
x\in\partial D
if there is a neighborhood
U
of
x
and a strictly
k
-pseudoconvex function
\varphi
on
U
such that
D\cap U=\{\varphi(x)<\varphi(x)\}
(cf.
[
23
, §2.2]
). In this sence, the
L^{1}
-norm function is strictly
(3g-2)
-pseudoconvex on the principal stratum and the unit ball bundle
\mathcal{Q}_{g}^{1}=\{q\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}\mid\boldsymbol{n}(q)<1\}
is
(3g-2)
-convex at each generic boundary point.

Dumas
[
15
]
observes that the complex Hessian (Levi form) of the
L^{1}
-norm function is positive definite at
q_{0}
along
\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
.
From an observation in the simplest case given in§
4.4.1
, we pose the following conjecture:

Conjecture 11.1.1
.

When
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}
is generic, the Levi form of the
L^{1}
-norm function is negative on a horizontal lift
T^{H}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
.

Conjecture 11.1.2
.

The Teichmüller metric is (strictly) plurisubharmonic on
T\mathcal{T}_{g}
.

Conjecture

11.1.1
is possibly rephrased for general
q\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}
.

11.2.
Royden’s theorem revisited

We first discuss the regularity of the
L^{1}
-norm function with our formula.
Namely, the continuity of the total differential
d\boldsymbol{n}
is derived from the presentation (
11.1.1
) as follows.

Let
\{[X_{n},\varphi_{n}]_{q_{n}}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence in
T\mathcal{Q}_{g}
converges to
[X_{0},\varphi_{0}]_{q_{0}}
with
q_{0}\neq 0
.
from the definition of the tangent vectors to
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
(§
5.6.2
), we can take an appropriate cochain
\xi_{n}
with
\delta\xi_{n}=X_{n}
(
n=0,1,\cdots
) such that
(\varphi_{n})_{i}
and
(\xi_{n})_{i}
and their derivatives converge to
(\varphi_{0})_{i}
on
(\xi_{0})_{i}
and their derivatives uniformly on any compact sets on
U_{i}
with suitable covering
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
after trivializing locally the universal family over the Teichmüller space (see the discussion in §
8.1.1
). Therefore,

D\boldsymbol{n}([X_{n},\varphi_{n}]_{q_{n}})
tends to
D\boldsymbol{n}([X_{0},\varphi_{0}]_{q_{0}})
as
n\to\infty

by Lebesgue theorem. This implies the continuity of
D\boldsymbol{n}
on
T\mathcal{Q}_{g}
.

The derivative
D\boldsymbol{n}
presents the
\partial
-derivative of the
L^{1}
-norm function (see
Corollary

11.1.1
). Since the
L^{1}
-norm function
\boldsymbol{n}
is real-valued,
\overline{\partial}\boldsymbol{n}=\overline{\partial\boldsymbol{n}}
holds as the identify for sections to the complexified cotangent space

(T^{*})^{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}=(T^{*})^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}\otimes\mathbb{C}=(T^{*})^{1,0}\mathcal{Q}_{g}\oplus(T^{*})^{0,1}\mathcal{Q}_{g}\to\mathcal{Q}_{g}^{\times}=\mathcal{Q}_{g}-\{0\},

where “
0
” means the zero section of the bundle
\mathcal{Q}_{g}\to\mathcal{T}_{g}
.
As a consequence, we conclude that the total differential
d\boldsymbol{n}=\partial\boldsymbol{n}+\overline{\partial}\boldsymbol{n}
is a continuous section on
\mathcal{Q}_{g}^{\times}
.

For the record, by combining with
Proposition

3.2.1
, we summarize as follows (cf.
[
68
, Lemma 2]
).

Corollary 11.2.1

(Royden)
.

The
L^{1}
-norm function
\boldsymbol{n}
on
\mathcal{Q}_{g}^{\times}=\mathcal{Q}_{g}-\{0\}
is strictly convex and of class
C^{1}
.

As noted by Royden in
[
68
]
, the regularity of the Teichmüller metric follows from the regularity of the
L^{1}
-norm and the following criterion (cf.
[
68
, Lemma]
). For the convenience of readers, we give a proof of the criterion at §
13.3
.

We recall that a function
G
on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
is said to be
complex homogeneous
if
G(\alpha\eta)=|\alpha|G(\eta)
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
and
\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
(See §
1
).

Proposition 11.2.1

(Royden)
.

Let
U
be an open subset of
\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
G(x,\eta)
a continuous function on
U\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
, which for each
x
is a positive convex, and complex homogeneous function in
\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Define
F(x,\xi)
on
U\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
by

F(x,\xi)=\sup\{{\rm Re}(\eta(\xi))\mid G(x,\eta)=1\}

where
\eta(\xi)
denotes the
\mathbb{C}
-bilinear pairing

\eta(\xi)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}\xi_{i}.

Then,
F
is continuous on
U\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and
F(x,\cdot)
is a positive convex complex homogeneous function in
\xi\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
for each
x\in U
. In addition,

•

for
x\in U
, if
G(x,\cdot)
is of class
C^{1}
on
\mathbb{C}^{n}-\{0\}
, then
F(x,\cdot)
is strictly convex; and

•

if
G(x,\cdot)
is strictly convex for each
x\in U
, and
G
is totally differentiable in the variable
x\in U
and the
x
-derivative is continuous on
U\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}-\{0\})
,
F
is of class
C^{1}
on
U\times(\mathbb{C}^{n}-\{0\})
. In fact, when

G(x+\Delta x,\eta)=G(x,\eta)+A_{x,\eta}(\Delta x)+\overline{A_{x,\eta}(\Delta x)}+o(|\Delta x|)

as
|\Delta x|\to 0
,

\displaystyle F(x+\Delta x,\xi+\Delta\xi)

\displaystyle=-F(x,\xi)(A_{x,\eta_{x,\xi}}(\Delta x)+\overline{A_{x,\eta_{x,\xi}}(\Delta x)})

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{1}{2}\eta_{x,\xi}(\Delta\xi)+\dfrac{1}{2}\overline{\eta_{x,\xi}(\Delta\xi)}+o(|\Delta x|+|\Delta\xi|)

as
|\Delta x|+|\Delta\xi|\to 0
, where
\eta_{x,\xi}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
is a unique vector satisfying
F(x,\xi)={\rm Re}(\eta_{x,\xi}(\xi))
and
G(x,\eta_{x,\xi})=1
.

Actually, Royden stated the proposition for real Finsler metrics. We discuss in complex analytic case for the sake of (more) direct applications to our purpose. However, the proof is essentially same as that of the real case. In summary, we obtain

Corollary 11.2.2

(Royden)
.

The Teichmüller metric
\boldsymbol{\tau}
on
T\mathcal{T}_{g}-\{0\}
is strictly convex and of class
C^{1}
.

11.3.
Teichmüller metric

The Teichmüller metric is thought of as a function on the tangent bundle:

T\mathcal{T}_{g}\ni v\mapsto\boldsymbol{\tau}(\pi_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}(v),v)

where
\pi_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}\colon T\mathcal{T}_{g}\to\mathcal{T}_{g}
is the projection.
(cf. §
3.2.4
).
In this section, we compute the differential of the Teichmüller metric
on
T\mathcal{T}_{g}
under our setting.

11.3.1.
Variational formula

We prove the following.

Theorem 11.3.1

(Derivative of the Teichmüller metric)
.

Let
x_{0}=(M_{0},f_{0})\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
v_{0}\in T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
.
Let
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a locally finite covering of
M_{0}
such that each
U_{i}
is a closed (topological) disk with smooth boundary in
M_{0}
.
Suppose thar
v_{0}
is represented by the Teichmüller differential
\boldsymbol{\tau}(x_{0},v_{0})\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}
for
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{M_{0}}-\{0\}
.
Then, for
V=\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]\cong T_{v_{0}}T\mathcal{T}_{g}
,

\displaystyle D\boldsymbol{\tau}|_{v_{0}}[V]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{4i\|q_{0}\|}\iint_{M_{0}}\left(\dot{\nu}_{i}q_{0}+\mu L_{\eta_{i}}(q_{0})-L_{\eta_{i}}(\mu q_{0})\right)d\overline{z}\wedge dz.

where

•

q_{0}\in H^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes 2})=\mathcal{Q}_{M_{0}}
;

•

Y\in Z^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
with
[Y]=v_{0}
in
H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\cong T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
;

•

\xi
,
\eta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{C}^{2,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\xi=X
and
\delta\eta=Y
;
and

•

\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}^{Dol}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]
with
\mathscr{D}_{0}(\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}})=\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}
and
\mu=-\overline{\partial}\xi_{i}
on
U_{i}
.

Proof.

By considering
q_{0}/\|q_{0}\|
instead of
q_{0}
in the statement, we may assume that
\|q_{0}\|=1
.
From the discussion in §
5.8
, we may also assume that

(\eta_{i})_{\overline{z}}=-\boldsymbol{\tau}(x_{0},v_{0})\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}

on
U_{i}
.
Consider a non-negative
C^{1}
-function

(11.3.1)

\displaystyle F(v,q)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\tau}(\pi_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}(v),v)\boldsymbol{n}(q)-{\rm Re}\mathscr{P}(v,q)

on the Whitney sum

T\mathcal{T}_{g}\oplus T^{*}\mathcal{T}_{g}
.
From the assumption, by the Teichmüller theorem,
F
attains the minimum
F(v_{0},q_{0})=0
at
(v_{0},q_{0})
. For the simplicity we let
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\tau}(\pi_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}(v_{0}),v_{0})
.

Since
F
attains the minumum at
(v_{0},q_{0})
(
\|q_{0}\|=1
) and the pairing function
\mathscr{P}
is holomorphic,

0=DF[V_{1},V_{2}]=D\boldsymbol{\tau}|_{v_{0}}[V_{1}]+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}D\boldsymbol{n}|_{q_{0}}[V_{2}]-\dfrac{1}{2}\left.D\mathscr{P}\right|_{(v_{0},q_{0})}\left[V_{1},V_{2}\right]

for all
(V_{1},V_{2})\in T_{(v_{0},q_{0})}(T\mathcal{T}_{g}\oplus T^{*}\mathcal{T}_{g})
.
From
Corollary

9.2.1
and
Theorem

11.1.1
,

\displaystyle D\boldsymbol{\tau}|_{v_{0}}[V_{1}]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\left.D\mathscr{P}\right|_{(v_{0},q_{0})}\left[V_{1},V_{2}\right]-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}D\boldsymbol{n}|_{q_{0}}[V_{2}]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{4i}\iint_{M_{0}}\left(\left((\dot{\nu}_{i}-[\xi_{i},\eta_{i}]_{\overline{z}})q_{0}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\varphi_{i}\right)-L_{\xi_{i}}\left(\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}q_{0}\right)\right)d\overline{z}\wedge dz

\displaystyle\qquad-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}\dfrac{1}{4i}\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\left(\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})\right)d\overline{z}\wedge dz

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{4i}\iint_{M_{0}}\left(\left((\dot{\nu}_{i}-[\xi_{i},\eta_{i}]_{\overline{z}})q_{0}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\varphi_{i}\right)-\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}\overline{q_{0}}}{2|q_{0}|}L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})\right)d\overline{z}\wedge dz

\displaystyle\qquad-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\left(\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})\right)d\overline{z}\wedge dz\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{4i}\iint_{M_{0}}\left((\dot{\nu}_{i}-[\xi_{i},\eta_{i}]_{\overline{z}})q_{0}+\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}\overline{q_{0}}}{2|q_{0}|}L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})\right)d\overline{z}\wedge dz.

Since
[\xi_{i},\eta_{i}]_{\overline{z}}=-[\mu,\eta_{i}]+[\xi_{i},(\eta_{i})_{\overline{z}}]
and

\displaystyle[\mu,\eta_{i}]q_{0}

\displaystyle=(\mu(\eta_{i})_{z}-\mu_{z}\eta_{i})q_{0}

\displaystyle=\mu(\eta_{i})_{z}q_{0}+\mu(\eta_{i})_{z}q_{0}+\eta_{i}\mu(q_{0})^{\prime}-L_{\eta_{i}}(\mu q_{0})

\displaystyle=\mu L_{\eta_{i}}(q_{0})-L_{\eta_{i}}(\mu q_{0})

\displaystyle[\xi_{i},(\eta_{i})_{\overline{z}}]q_{0}

\displaystyle=\left(\xi_{i}(\eta_{i})_{\overline{z}z}-(\xi_{i})_{z}(\eta_{i})_{\overline{z}}\right)q_{0}

\displaystyle=\xi_{i}\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}\overline{q_{0}}}{2|q_{0}|}(q_{0})^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}(\xi_{i})_{z}|q_{0}|=\dfrac{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}\overline{q_{0}}}{2|q_{0}|}L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0}),

we obtain

\displaystyle D\boldsymbol{\tau}|_{v_{0}}[V_{1}]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{4i}\iint_{M_{0}}\left(\dot{\nu}_{i}q_{0}+\mu L_{\eta_{i}}(q_{0})-L_{\eta_{i}}(\mu q_{0})\right)d\overline{z}\wedge dz,

which is what we wanted.
∎

Hence, from
Theorem

5.5.1
, we conclude the following
(cf.
Remark

11.1.1
).

Corollary 11.3.1

(
\partial
-differential of the Teichmüller metric)
.

Let
v_{0}=[Y]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\cong T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
.
Suppose that
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}=\boldsymbol{\tau}(\pi_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}(v_{0}),v_{0})
and
v_{0}
is represented by
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}\overline{q_{0}}/|q_{0}|
, where
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}-\{0\}
. Then,

\partial\boldsymbol{\tau}|_{v_{0}}=\dfrac{1}{2\|q_{0}\|}\left\lsem L_{\eta_{i}}(q_{0}),q_{0}\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\in\mathbb{T}^{\dagger}_{[Y]}[\mathcal{U}]\cong T^{*}_{v_{0}}T\mathcal{T}_{g}

as a
1
-form on
T\mathcal{T}_{g}
,
where
\eta=\{\eta_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{C}^{2,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}-{\rm Zero}(q_{0})}))
with
\delta\eta=Y
and
(\eta_{i})_{\overline{z}}=-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{0}\overline{q_{0}}/|q_{0}|
.

Corollary

11.1.1
is equivalent to

\partial\boldsymbol{\tau}\left(\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\right)=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{TT}}\left(\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}},\dfrac{1}{2\|q_{0}\|}\left\lsem L_{\eta_{i}}(q_{0}),q_{0}\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y]}}\right)

for
Y=\delta\eta
,
\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}\in\mathbb{T}_{Y}[\mathcal{U}]\cong T_{v_{0}}T\mathcal{T}_{g}
,
\left\langlebar\mu,\dot{\nu}\right\ranglebar^{\scriptscriptstyle cycl}_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}}=\mathscr{D}_{0}(\left\lsem X,\dot{Y}\right\rsem_{{\scriptscriptstyle Y}})
, and
v_{0}=\mathscr{T}_{x_{0}}([Y])
.

Remark 11.3.1
.

From
Remark

9.2.1
and Remark (
5
) after the statement of
Theorem

11.1.1
, when
[X]=0
,
\dot{\nu}=\dot{\nu}_{i}
can be assumed to be a globally defined Beltrami differential on
M_{0}
and the formula (
11.3.1
) becomes

D\boldsymbol{\tau}|_{v_{0}}[V]=\dfrac{1}{4i\|q_{0}\|}\iint_{M_{0}}\dot{\nu}_{i}(z)q_{0}(z)d\overline{z}\wedge dz=\dfrac{1}{2}\iint_{M_{0}}\dot{\nu}(z)\dfrac{q_{0}(z)}{\|q_{0}\|}dxdy=\dfrac{1}{2}\langle\dot{\nu},q_{0}/\|q_{0}\|\rangle.

In particular, in this case, we derive the following formula:

(11.3.2)

\left.\dfrac{d}{dt}\kappa(x_{0},v_{0}+tV)\right|_{t=0}=2{\rm Re}\left(D\boldsymbol{\tau}|_{v_{0}}[V]\right)={\rm Re}\langle V,q_{0}/\|q_{0}\|\rangle,

which is probably well-known to experts
(see
[
62
, Lemma 2.1]
).

Remark 11.3.2
.

In
Theorem

11.3.1
, the condition of the cochain
\eta
can be weakened.
Indeed, we may consider the cochain
\eta\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{C}^{2,1}(\Omega_{M_{0}}))
with only the condition
\delta\eta=Y
by applying the argument for the independence of choices of representatives for defining the model of pairing given in §
5.5.1
.

11.4.
Infinitesimal Duality

From the definition, the Teichmüller metric is thought of as the dual (Finsler) metric on
\mathcal{T}_{g}
to the
L^{1}
-norm on
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
(cf. §
3.2.4
).
The above mentioned variational formulae of the
L^{1}
-norm and the Teichmüller metric implies that these also satisfy the following duality in the infinitesimal sense:

Theorem 11.4.1

(Infinitesimal duality)
.

Let
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
and let
v_{0}\in T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
which is presented by the Teichmüller Beltrami differential
\|q_{0}\|\overline{q}/|q|
. Then,
the
\partial
-derivatives of the squares of the
L^{1}
-norm and the Teichmüller metric satsify

(11.4.1)

{\bf Swh}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}\circ({\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}})^{-1}(-\partial\boldsymbol{n}^{2}|_{q_{0}})={\bf Swh}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}\left(-\dfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{X}_{\boldsymbol{n}^{2}}|_{q_{0}}\right)=\partial\boldsymbol{\tau}^{2}|_{v_{0}},

where
\mathscr{X}_{\boldsymbol{n}^{2}}
is the Hamiltonian vector field of the square
\boldsymbol{n}^{2}
of the
L^{1}
-norm function.

Proof.

Let
\eta=\{\eta_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the cochain with
(\eta_{i})_{\overline{z}}=-\overline{q_{0}}/|q_{0}|
.
Let
Y=\delta\eta
.
Notice that

(11.4.2)

\boldsymbol{\tau}(x_{0},v_{0})=\sup_{\|q\|=1,q\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}}{\rm Re}(\langle v_{0},q\rangle)=\sup_{\|q\|=1,q\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}}{\rm Re}\left(\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}q\right)=\|q_{0}\|.

Since a cochain
\eta^{\prime}=\|q_{0}\|\eta
and
Y^{\prime}=\|q_{0}\|Y
satisfies

\delta\eta^{\prime}=\|q_{0}\|Y=Y^{\prime},\quad(\eta^{\prime}_{i})_{\overline{z}}=-\|q_{0}\|\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}=-\boldsymbol{\tau}(x_{0},v_{0})\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|},

v_{0}\in T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
is the tangent vector presented by

\boldsymbol{\tau}(x_{0},v_{0})\overline{q_{0}}/|q_{0}|
and
[Y^{\prime}]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
.
From
Corollary

11.1.1
and
Corollary

11.3.1
,

\displaystyle-\partial\boldsymbol{n}^{2}|_{q_{0}}

\displaystyle=-2\boldsymbol{n}(q_{0})\partial\boldsymbol{n}|_{q_{0}}=\|q_{0}\|[L_{\eta}(q_{0}),-Y]_{q_{0}}

\displaystyle=[L_{\|q_{0}\|\eta}(q_{0}),-\|q_{0}\|Y]_{q_{0}}

\displaystyle=[L_{\eta^{\prime}}(q_{0}),-Y^{\prime}]_{q_{0}}

\displaystyle\partial\boldsymbol{\tau}^{2}|_{v_{0}}

\displaystyle=2\boldsymbol{\tau}(x_{0},v_{0})\partial\boldsymbol{\tau}|_{v_{0}}=2\|q_{0}\|\partial\boldsymbol{\tau}|_{v_{0}}

\displaystyle=\left\lsem L_{\eta^{\prime}}(q_{0}),q_{0}\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y^{\prime}]}}

in the models.
From
Theorem

10.3.1
and
Proposition

10.4.1
,
we obtain

\displaystyle{\bf Swh}_{\mathbb{T}}\circ({\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}\left([L_{\eta^{\prime}}(q_{0}),-Y^{\prime}]_{q_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle={\bf Swh}_{\mathbb{T}}\left([Y^{\prime},L_{\eta^{\prime}}(q_{0})]_{q_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\lsem L_{\eta^{\prime}}(q_{0}),q_{0}\right\rsem^{\dagger}_{{\scriptscriptstyle[Y^{\prime}]}}.

From
Proposition

2.6.4
,

({\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}\left(-\partial\boldsymbol{n}^{2}|_{q_{0}}\right)=\dfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{X}_{-\boldsymbol{n}^{2}}|_{q_{0}}=-\dfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{X}_{\boldsymbol{n}^{2}}|_{q_{0}}.

Since the model spaces and the model bundle maps actually describe the actual spaces and the actual bundle maps (cf.§
9.3
),
we obtain the formula which we wanted.
∎

From the calculation in the proof of
Theorem

11.4.1
, we also obtain the following.

Corollary 11.4.1

(Canonical sections)
.

We have

\displaystyle D\Pi^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}\left(({\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{T}})^{-1}\left(-\partial\boldsymbol{n}^{2}|_{q_{0}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=D\Pi^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}\left(-\dfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{X}_{\boldsymbol{n}^{2}}|_{q_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}\left(\partial\boldsymbol{\tau}^{2}|_{v_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=v_{0}=\left[\|q_{0}\|\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\right]\in T\mathcal{T}_{g}

for
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}
, where
\Pi_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}\colon T\mathcal{T}_{g}\to\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
\Pi^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}\colon\mathcal{Q}_{g}\to\mathcal{T}_{g}
are the projections of the bundles,
D\Pi^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}\colon T\mathcal{Q}_{g}\to T\mathcal{T}_{g}
is the differential.

Chapter 12
Teichmüller Beltrami differentials

In this chapter, we discuss the maps

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{Q}_{g}^{\times}:=\mathcal{Q}_{g}-\{0\}\ni q\mapsto\|q\|\left[\dfrac{\overline{q}}{|q|}\right]\in T^{\times}\mathcal{T}_{g}=T\mathcal{T}_{g}-\{0\}

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{Q}_{g}^{\times}\ni q\mapsto\left[\dfrac{\overline{q}}{|q|}\right]\in T^{\times}\mathcal{T}_{g}

defined by the Teichmüller Beltrami differentials (§
3.2.3
),
where “
0
” in the equation means the zero sections.
We call the maps the
Teichmüller Beltrami maps
.
From the Teichmüller theorem, the map
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}
is continuous, and the map
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}
is a homeomorphism.
The Teichmüller Beltrami maps satisfy

(12.0.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tau}\left(\Pi_{T\mathcal{T}_{g}}^{\dagger}(q_{0}),\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}(q_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{n}(q_{0})

(12.0.2)

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}(q_{0})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{n}(q_{0})^{-1}\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}(q_{0})

for
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}

(cf. (
11.4.2
)).

The aim of this chapter is to prove
Theorem

A
.

12.1.
Teichmüller Beltrami maps are real-analytic on strata

We first discuss the smoothness of the Teichmüller Beltrami maps
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}
and
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}
.
From
Corollary

11.4.1
, we have the following commutative diagram:

\displaystyle{T\mathcal{Q}_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D\Pi^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}}
\displaystyle{T\mathcal{T}_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}}
\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}^{\times}_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{X}_{\boldsymbol{n}^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}}
\scriptstyle{\Pi^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}}
\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}_{g}.}

Notice that
-\mathscr{X}_{\boldsymbol{n}^{2}}=2{\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}(-\partial\boldsymbol{n}^{2})
from
Proposition

2.6.4
, and the bundle map
{\boldsymbol{dual}}^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}\colon T\mathcal{Q}_{g}\to T^{*}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
is biholomorphic.
Since the
L^{1}
-norm function
\boldsymbol{n}
on
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
is real-analytic on each stratum of
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
, so is
\partial\boldsymbol{n}^{2}
(cf. §
3.4
).
Therefore,

\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}=D\Pi_{T\mathcal{T}_{g}}^{\dagger}\circ\left(-\dfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{X}_{\boldsymbol{n}^{2}}\right)

is real-analytc on each stratum.
Since
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}=\boldsymbol{n}^{-1}\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}
,

\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}
is also real-analytic on each stratum.
We summarize as follows.

Proposition 12.1.1

(Teichmüller Beltrami maps are real-analytic)
.

The Teichmüller Beltrami maps
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}
,
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}\colon\mathcal{Q}^{\times}_{g}\to T^{\times}\mathcal{T}_{g}
are real-analytic on each stratum of
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
.

12.2.
Teichmüller Beltrami map is diffeomorphic on strata

We deal with the following map defined by the pairing:

(12.2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{g}^{*}\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{g}\ni(q,\alpha)\mapsto\mathbf{TB}(q,\alpha)=\|q\|\iint_{M}\dfrac{\overline{q}}{|q|}\alpha=\dfrac{\|q\|}{2i}\iint_{M}\dfrac{\overline{q(z)}}{|q(z)|}\alpha(z)d\overline{z}\wedge dz

(12.2.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{g}^{*}\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{g}\ni(q,\alpha)\mapsto\mathbf{TB}_{0}(q,\alpha)=\iint_{M}\dfrac{\overline{q}}{|q|}\alpha=\dfrac{1}{2i}\iint_{M}\dfrac{\overline{q(z)}}{|q(z)|}\alpha(z)d\overline{z}\wedge dz,

where
M
is the underlying surface of
q
(and
\alpha
). The map (
12.2.2
) is decomposed into

(12.2.3)

\begin{CD}\mathcal{Q}_{g}^{*}\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{g}@>{\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}\oplus id}>{}>T\mathcal{T}_{g}\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{g}@>{\mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}}>{}>\mathbb{C}\\
(q,\alpha)@>{}>{}>\left(\left[\dfrac{\overline{q}}{|q|}\right],\alpha\right)\\
(v,\alpha)@>{}>{}>\langle v,\alpha\rangle.\end{CD}

12.2.1.
Variational formula

Let
\{(q_{t},\alpha_{t})\}_{|t|<\epsilon}
be a holomorphic curve in
\mathcal{Q}^{*}_{g}\oplus\mathcal{Q}_{g}
.
Let
V_{1}=[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}
and
V_{2}=[X,\psi]_{\alpha_{0}}
be the tangent vectors in

{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})\cong T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
and

{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{0}})\cong T_{\alpha_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
,
where
\mathcal{U}=\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a locally finite covering of the underlying surface
M_{0}
of
q_{0}
(and
\alpha_{0}
) with
H^{1}(U_{i},\Theta_{M_{0}})=0
for
i\in I
.
Then,

\displaystyle\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}+t\varphi+o(t)}}{|q_{0}+t\varphi+o(t)|}(\alpha_{0}+t\psi+o(t))

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\alpha_{0}+t\left(-\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}\alpha_{0}}{2q_{0}|q_{0}|}\varphi_{i}+\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\psi_{i}\right)+\overline{t}\dfrac{\alpha_{0}\overline{\varphi_{i}}}{2|q_{0}|}+o(t)

on any compact set in
U_{i}
(
i\in I
) outside of the zeros of
q_{0}
, where
\xi=\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{C}^{1,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\xi=X
.
From
Proposition

5.6.2
, the above calculation also works even when the family
\{q_{t}\}_{|t<\epsilon}
is contained in a stratum in
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
. Thus, we obtain the following.

Proposition 12.2.1

(Differential of Teichmüller Beltrami differentials)
.

Under the above notation,
when
q_{0}
and the family
\{q_{t}\}_{|t|<\epsilon}
are in a stratum
\mathcal{Q}_{g}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n};\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle D\mathbf{TB}_{0}|_{(q_{0},\alpha_{0})}[V_{1},V_{2}]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2i}\iint_{M_{0}}\left(-\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}\alpha_{0}}{2q_{0}|q_{0}|}(\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0}))+\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}(\psi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(\alpha_{0}))\right)d\overline{z}\wedge dz

\displaystyle\overline{D}\mathbf{TB}_{0}|_{(q_{0},\alpha_{0})}[V_{1},V_{2}]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{4i}\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\alpha_{0}}{|q_{0}|}\overline{\left(\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})\right)}d\overline{z}\wedge dz

for
V_{1}=[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}\in T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n};\epsilon)

and
V_{2}=[X,\psi]_{\alpha_{0}}\in T_{\alpha_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
.

Before proving
Proposition

12.2.1
, we notice that the integrals in
Proposition

12.2.1
are independent of the choice of the cochain
\xi\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{C}^{1,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\xi=X
. From a bit modification of the argument in Remark (3) below
Theorem

11.1.1
, the independence follows from the following identities:

(12.2.4)

\displaystyle\left(-\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}\alpha_{0}}{2q_{0}|q_{0}|}L_{\chi}(q_{0})+\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}L_{\chi}(\alpha_{0})\right)d\overline{z}\wedge dz

\displaystyle=-d\left(\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}\alpha_{0}}{|q_{0}|}\chi d\overline{z}\right)

(12.2.5)

\displaystyle\dfrac{\overline{\alpha_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}L_{\chi}(q_{0})d\overline{z}\wedge dz

\displaystyle=-2d\left(\dfrac{q_{0}\overline{\alpha_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\chi d\overline{z}\right)

on
M_{0}-{\rm Zero}(q_{0})

for a global vector field
\chi
on
M_{0}
of class
\mathscr{C}^{1,1}
. We should be careful in the discussion because the
(0,1
)-differentials in the rights of the above two identities are discontinuous on
M_{0}
. However, as was discussed before, the Green formula varies for such differentials
since the differentials are bounded and continuous except for the zeros of
q_{0}

(cf. §
5.8
).

The differentiable formulae are obtained by
Proposition

9.1.1
with applying the argument in
Lemma

11.1.1

and the following identifies

\displaystyle L_{\xi_{i}}\left(\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\alpha_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}\alpha_{0}}{2q_{0}|q_{0}|}L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})+\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}L_{\xi_{i}}(\alpha_{0})

\displaystyle L_{\xi_{i}}\left(\overline{\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\alpha_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=L_{\xi_{i}}\left(\dfrac{q_{0}}{|q_{0}|}\overline{\alpha_{0}}\right)=\dfrac{\overline{\alpha_{0}}}{2|q_{0}|}L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})

on
U_{i}
for
i\in I
, which are equivalent to (
12.2.4
) and (
12.2.5
), respectively.

12.2.2.
Proof of
Theorem

A

From
Proposition

12.1.1
, we only discuss the regularity of the differentials of the Teichmüller Beltrami map
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}
.
Let
N_{0}
be a sufficiently small neighborhood of
x_{0}
in
\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
\{\alpha_{1}
,
\cdots
,
\alpha_{3g-3}\}
be a system of holomorphic sections of the bundle
\mathcal{Q}_{g}|_{N_{0}}\to N_{0}
such that
\alpha_{1}(x)
,
\cdots
,
\alpha_{3g-3}(x)
generate the fiber
\mathcal{Q}_{x}
for any
x\in N_{0}
.
Then, the map

T\mathcal{T}_{g}|_{N_{0}}\ni v\mapsto(x,(\langle v,\alpha_{1}(x)\rangle,\cdots,\langle v,\alpha_{3g-3}(x)\rangle))\in N_{0}\times\mathbb{C}^{3g-3}

(
x\in N_{0}
with
v\in T_{x}\mathcal{T}_{g}
) is a holomorphic (local) trivialization.

Case :
q_{0}
is generic

We first discuss the case where
q_{0}
is generic, i.e.
q_{0}
is in the principal stratum
\mathcal{Q}_{1}=\mathcal{Q}_{g}(1,\cdots,1;-1)
.

Let
V=[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}\in{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})
.
Let
V=[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}\in{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})
. Let
\xi\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathcal{A}^{0,0}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
with
\delta\xi=X
. Let
\{q_{t}\}_{|t|<\epsilon}
be a holomorphic family of holomorphic quadratic differentials which is tangent to
V
at
t=0
. Let
x(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{g}
with
q_{t}\in\mathcal{Q}_{x(t)}
(
x(0)=x_{0}
). Let
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{i}(x_{0})
and
V_{k}=[X,\psi^{k}]_{\alpha_{k}}\in{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{k}})
(
k=1
,
\cdots
,
3g-3
) the tangent vector to the family
\{\alpha_{k}(x(t))\}_{|t|<\epsilon}
at
t=0
.

Suppose
V=[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}\in{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})
satisfies

\displaystyle D\mathbf{TB}|_{(q_{0},\alpha_{k})}[V,V_{k}]+\overline{D}\mathbf{TB}|_{(q_{0},\alpha_{k})}[V,V_{k}]

\displaystyle=D\boldsymbol{n}(q_{0})|_{q_{0}}[V]\mathbf{TB}_{0}(q_{0},\alpha_{k})+\boldsymbol{n}(q_{0})D\mathbf{TB}_{0}|_{(q_{0},\alpha_{k})}[V,V_{k}]

\displaystyle\quad+\overline{D\boldsymbol{n}(q_{0})|_{q_{0}}[V]}\mathbf{TB}_{0}(q_{0},\alpha_{k})+\boldsymbol{n}(q_{0})\overline{D}\mathbf{TB}_{0}|_{(q_{0},\alpha_{k})}[V,V_{k}]=0

for
k=1
,
\cdots
,
3g-3
(cf.
Lemma

9.1.1
).
These equations are equivalent to

(12.2.6)

\displaystyle\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}\alpha_{k}}{|q_{0}|}\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{2|q_{0}|}\left(\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})\right)+\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}\alpha_{k}}{|q_{0}|}\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{q_{0}}{2|q_{0}|}\overline{\left(\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})\right)}

\displaystyle=-\iint_{M_{0}}|q_{0}|\iint_{M_{0}}\left(-\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}\alpha_{k}}{2q_{0}|q_{0}|}(\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0}))+\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}(\psi^{k}_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(\alpha_{k}))\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\iint_{M_{0}}|q_{0}|\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\alpha_{k}}{2|q_{0}|}\overline{\left(\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})\right)}

for
k=1
,
\cdots
,
3g-3
, where we omit to write the Euclid measure
d\overline{z}\wedge dz/2i
in the equation for the simplicity.
Since we now discuss about the non-degeneracy of the differential of the Teichmüller Beltrami map, we may further suppose that these equations hold no matter how we choose initially the sections
\alpha_{k}
around
x_{0}
.

Suppose to the contrary that
[X]\neq 0
. Take
a\in\mathbb{C}
arbitrary.
By definition,
D\alpha_{k}|_{x_{0}}[[X]]=V_{k}=[X,\psi^{k}]_{\alpha_{k}}
.
Since
[X,\psi^{k}+\beta]_{\alpha_{k}}\in{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{k}})
for
\beta\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
, by modifying the sections
\alpha_{k}
in the vertical direction in the beginning, we may assume that

(12.2.7)

\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}(\psi_{i}^{k}-L_{\xi_{i}}(\alpha_{k}))=a

for
k=1
,
\cdots
,
3g-3
.
However, this contradicts to (
12.2.6
) because the terms except for the second in the first term of the right-hand side are independent of
a\in\mathbb{C}
. Therefore,
[X]=0
and
V_{k}=0
for
k=1
,
\cdots
,
3g-3
. Hence, we may also assume that
\xi\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})

and
\psi^{k}=L_{\xi}(q_{0})
.

By substituting
\beta=\varphi_{i}-L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
into (
12.2.6
), we obtain

(12.2.8)

\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}\gamma}{|q_{0}|}{\rm Re}\left(\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\beta\right)=-\iint_{M_{0}}|q_{0}|\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\gamma}{2|q_{0}|}\left(\overline{\beta}-\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{q_{0}}\beta\right)

for all
\gamma\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
since
\{\alpha_{k}\}_{k=1}^{3g-3}
is a basis of
\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
.
By substituting
\gamma=q_{0}
into (
12.2.8
), we get

(12.2.9)

\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{|q_{0}|}\beta=\overline{\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{q_{0}}{|q_{0}|}\overline{\beta}}=0.

Suppose to the contrary that
\beta\neq 0
. By substituting
\gamma=\beta
into (
12.2.8
), we get

0=\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\beta}{|q_{0}|}\left(\overline{\beta}-\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{q_{0}}\beta\right)=\iint_{M_{0}}\left(1-\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{q_{0}}\dfrac{\beta}{\overline{\beta}}\right)\dfrac{|\beta|^{2}}{|q_{0}|}.

Since
{\rm Re}(1-(\overline{q_{0}}/q_{0})(\beta/\overline{\beta}))\geq 0
, we obtain

1-\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}}{q_{0}}\dfrac{\beta}{\overline{\beta}}=0

almost everywhere on
M_{0}
. Since
q_{0}
and
\beta
are holomorphic quadratic differentials, the equation means that the imaginary part of a meromorphic function
\beta/q_{0}
vanishes. Therefore, we obtain
\beta=\lambda q_{0}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. On the other hand, from (
12.2.9
),
\lambda=0
and
\beta=0
. This is a contradiction. Thus, we conclude that
V=[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}=[\delta\xi,L_{\xi}(q_{0})]_{q_{0}}=0
in
{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{q_{0}})
.

Case :
q_{0}
is in another stratum

The strategy of the proof of the non-degeneracy is same. We give a sketch by comparing with the discussion of the previous case.

Suppose
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{g}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n};\epsilon)
. We denote by
\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}(q_{0})
the subspace of
\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
consisting of
\psi\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
such that
\psi/q_{0}
is at most simple poles on
M_{0}
. Dumas
[
15
, §5]
shows that
\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}(q_{0})
is thought of as the tangent space at
q_{0}
to the stratum containing
q_{0}
of a natural stratification on
\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
induced from the stratification on
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
. In particular, for
\psi\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}(q_{0})
,
[0,\psi]_{q_{0}}\in T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n};\epsilon)
. Notice that
q_{0}\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}(q_{0})
and hence
\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}(q_{0})
is not trivial.
Dumas also discuss an Hermitian inner product

(12.2.10)

\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{\alpha}\beta}{|q_{0}|}\quad(\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}(q_{0}))

on
\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}(q_{0})
. Let
d_{0}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}(q_{0})
.

From the beginning of the proof in the previous discussion, we modify the basis
\{\alpha_{k}\}_{k=1}^{3g-3}
such that
\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}(q_{0})
for
k=1
,
\cdots
,
d_{0}
. Furthermore,
V_{k}\in T_{\alpha_{k}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n};\epsilon)
for
k=1
,
\cdots
,
d_{0}
.

Let
V=[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}\in T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n};\epsilon)
, and suppose

\displaystyle D\mathbf{TB}|_{(q_{0},\alpha_{k})}[V,V_{k}]+\overline{D}\mathbf{TB}|_{(q_{0},\alpha_{k})}[V,V_{k}]=0

for
k=1
,
\cdots
,
d_{0}
. Take an arbitrary
a\in\mathbb{C}
. From
Proposition

5.6.2
and the above discussion,
[X,\psi^{k}+\beta]_{\alpha_{k}}\in T_{\alpha_{k}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n};\epsilon)
for
\beta\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}(q_{0})
. Since the stratum
\mathcal{Q}_{g}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n};\epsilon)
is a complex manifold, after modifying the analytic disk
\{\alpha_{k}(x(t))\}_{|t|<\epsilon}
in the vertical direction in the stratum, we may assume that (
12.2.7
) holds for
k=1
,
\cdots
,
d_{0}
. Hence, we get
X=\delta\xi
for some
\xi\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
and
V_{k}=[X,\psi^{k}]_{\alpha_{k}}=0
in
{\bf H}^{1}(\mathcal{U},\mathbb{L}_{\alpha_{k}})
. By thinking the Hermitian product (
12.2.10
) on
\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}(q_{0})
, the rest of the discussion is almost the same as that given in the case where
q_{0}
is generic to obtain
V=[X,\varphi]_{q_{0}}=0
.

12.3.
Conjectural picture on unit sphere bundles

Notice from (
1.2.2
) that the Teichmüller Beltrami map
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}
is a bundle isomorphism from the unit sphere bundle
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}_{g}
to the unit sphere bundle
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{T}_{g}=\{v\in T\mathcal{T}_{g}\mid\boldsymbol{\tau}(\Pi_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}(v),v)=1\}
.
We discuss the Teichmüller Beltrami map
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}
in the infinitesimal level.
Here, we only treat the map
\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}
on the generic differentials. The purpose of this section is to make a conjectural picture of the Teichmüller Beltrami map from Complex analysis point of view.

Let
q_{0}\in\mathcal{SQ}_{g}
be a generic differential.
Let
x_{0}=\Pi^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}(q_{0})
.
As the proof of
Theorem

A
, let
\alpha_{k}
be a local holomorphic section of
T\mathcal{T}_{g}\to\mathcal{T}_{g}
defined on a neighborhood
U_{0}
of
x_{0}
such that
\{\alpha_{k}(x)\}_{k=1}^{3g-3}
generates
T_{x}\mathcal{T}_{g}
for
x\in U_{0}
.
Let
\mathbf{TB}_{0,k}(q)=\langle\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}_{0}(q),\alpha_{k}(\Pi^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}(q))\rangle
for
q\in(\Pi^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}})^{-1}(U_{0})
.
Then

(12.3.1)

\displaystyle(\Pi^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}_{g}})^{-1}(U_{0})\ni q\mapsto(\Pi_{\mathcal{T}_{g}}(\mathscr{T}\!\!\mathscr{B}(q)),(\mathbf{TB}_{1}(q),\cdots,\mathbf{TB}_{3g-3}(q)))\in U_{0}\times\mathbb{C}^{3g-3}

is a local trivialization.

12.3.1.
Horizontal lifts

Let
[X_{q_{0}}]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
which presents
\overline{q_{0}}/{|q_{0}|}
.
Take a guiding frame
\mathfrak{gf}
satisfying
\mathfrak{gf}([X_{q_{0}}])=\overline{q_{0}}/|q_{0}|
.
Let
\mathfrak{L}
be the good section defined from
\mathfrak{gf}
.
We assume that any
[X]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
is of form

(12.3.2)

\mathfrak{L}(X)=\alpha\mathfrak{L}(X_{q_{0}})+\beta\xi,

where
\alpha
,
\beta\in\mathbb{C}
and
\xi\in C^{0}(\mathcal{U},\mathscr{C}^{1,1}(\Theta_{M_{0}}))
.
For
[X]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
, we define a horizontall lift of
[X]
by

V^{H}([X])=[X,\{L_{\mathfrak{L}(X)_{i}}(q_{0})-Q_{X}\}_{i\in I}]_{q_{0}},

where
Q_{X}\in\Gamma(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes 2}))
satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}Q_{X}

\displaystyle=\overline{\partial}L_{\mathfrak{L}(X)_{i}}(q_{0})\quad(\mbox{on $U_{i}$})

(12.3.3)

\displaystyle\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{\varphi}}{|q_{0}|}Q_{X}

\displaystyle=0

for
i\in I
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
.
Though the uniqueness condition (
12.3.3
) is different from the condition given in (
11.1.3
),
we can also check that the map

T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}\cong H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})\ni[X]\mapsto V^{H}([X])\in T_{q_{0}}\mathcal{Q}_{g}

is a well-defined injective map (cf. §
11.1.3
). From the proof of
Proposition

11.1.1
, the image is thought of as a horizontal lift of
T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{T}_{g}
contained the real tangent space
T^{\mathbb{R}}_{q_{0}}\mathcal{SQ}_{g}
of
\mathcal{SQ}_{g}
at
q_{0}
.

The horizontal lift
V^{H}([X])
here is independent of the choice of the guiding frame. Indeed, let
\mathfrak{gf}^{\prime}
be another guiding frame with
\mathfrak{gf}^{\prime}([X_{q_{0}}])=\overline{q_{0}}/|q_{0}|
such that the good section
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}
defined from
\mathfrak{gf}^{\prime}
satisfies the condition (
12.3.2
). Let
[X]\in H^{1}(\mathcal{U},\Theta_{M_{0}})
and set
\xi=\mathfrak{L}(X)
and
\xi^{\prime}=\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}(X)
. Take
Q^{\prime}_{X}\in\Gamma(M_{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,1}(\Omega_{M_{0}}^{\otimes 2}))
defined from
\xi^{\prime}
with the uniqueness condition (
12.3.3
). Notice from
\delta\xi=\delta\xi^{\prime}=X
that the difference

(L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})-Q_{X})-(L_{\xi^{\prime}_{i}}(q_{0})-Q^{\prime}_{X})

is a holomorphic quadratic differential on
M_{0}
, and
\chi=\xi_{i}-\xi^{\prime}_{i}
on
U_{i}
is a global section (cf.
Proposition

6.1.1
).

Take an arbitrary
\varphi\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
. From the uniqueness condition (
12.3.3
),

\displaystyle\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{\varphi}}{|q_{0}|}((L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})-Q_{X})-(L_{\xi^{\prime}_{i}}(q_{0})-Q^{\prime}_{X}))=\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{\varphi}}{|q_{0}|}L_{\chi}(q_{0}).

Here, the integrand is

\displaystyle\dfrac{\overline{\varphi}}{|q_{0}|}L_{\chi}(q_{0})d\overline{z}\wedge dz

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\overline{\varphi}}{|q_{0}|}(q_{0}^{\prime}\chi+2q_{0}\xi_{z})d\overline{z}\wedge dz

\displaystyle=2\overline{\left(\dfrac{\varphi}{q_{0}}\right)}\left(\dfrac{\overline{q_{0}}q_{0}^{\prime}}{2|q_{0}|}\chi+|q_{0}|\chi_{z}\right)d\overline{z}\wedge dz

\displaystyle=-2d\left(\left(\overline{\left(\dfrac{\varphi}{q_{0}}\right)}|q_{0}|\chi\right)d\overline{z}\right),

which is the
d
-differential of a bounded
1
-form which is differentiable except for zeros of
q_{0}
. Therefore, by applying Royden’s argment (§
5.8
), we get

\displaystyle\iint_{M_{0}}\dfrac{\overline{\varphi}}{|q_{0}|}((L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})-Q_{X})-(L_{\xi^{\prime}_{i}}(q_{0})-Q^{\prime}_{X}))

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0}\iint_{M_{r}}\dfrac{\overline{\varphi}}{|q_{0}|}L_{\chi}(q_{0})=-2\lim_{r\to 0}\int_{\partial M_{r}}\overline{\varphi}\dfrac{q_{0}}{|q_{0}|}\chi\,d\overline{z}=0

by Green’s theorem, where
\{M_{r}\}_{r>0}
is an exhaustion of
M-{\rm Zero}(q_{0})
defined by removing
r
-disks (with respect to some local charts) around each zero of
q_{0}
.
Since
\varphi\in\mathcal{Q}_{x_{0}}
is taken arbitrary, we have that
L_{\xi_{i}}(q_{0})-Q_{X}=L_{\xi^{\prime}_{i}}(q_{0})-Q^{\prime}_{X}
on
U_{i}
for
i\in I
(cf.
[
15
, §5]
).

12.3.2.
Conjectural picture
